Efficient Sampling with Discrete Diffusion Models:
Sharp and Adaptive Guarantees
Daniil Dmitriev
† † thanks: Equal contribution, alphabetical order. † † thanks: Department of Statistics and Data Science, the Wharton School, University of Pennsylvania; email: {daniild,zhihanh,ytwei}@wharton.upenn.edu
Zhihan Huang *†
Yuting Wei †
August 24, 2026
Abstract
Diffusion models over discrete spaces have recently shown striking empirical success, yet their theoretical foundations remain incomplete. In this paper, we study the sampling efficiency of score-based discrete diffusion models under a continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC) formulation, with a focus on τ \tau -leaping-based samplers. We establish sharp
convergence guarantees for attaining ε \varepsilon accuracy in Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence for both uniform and masking noising processes.
For uniform discrete diffusion, we show that the τ \tau -leaping algorithm achieves an iteration complexity of order O ~ ​ ( d / ε ) \widetilde{O}(d/\varepsilon) , with d d the ambient dimension of the target distribution, eliminating linear dependence on the vocabulary size S S and improving existing bounds by a factor of d d ; moreover, we establish a matching algorithmic lower bound showing that linear dependence on the ambient dimension is unavoidable in general.
For masking discrete diffusion, we introduce a modified τ \tau -leaping sampler whose convergence rate is governed by an intrinsic information-theoretic quantity, termed the effective total correlation , which is bounded by d ​ log ⁡ S d\log S but can be sublinear or even constant for structured data. As a consequence, the sampler provably adapts to low-dimensional structure without prior knowledge or algorithmic modification, yielding sublinear convergence rates for various practical examples (such as hidden Markov models, image data, and random graphs). Our analysis requires no boundedness or smoothness assumptions on the score estimator beyond control of the score entropy loss.

1 Introduction
Diffusion models have recently emerged as state-of-the-art approaches for high-fidelity image generation and video synthesis ( Ho et al. (2020) ; Dhariwal and Nichol (2021) ; Song and Ermon (2019) ; Ho et al. (2022) ), and have already led to significant scientific advances in various domains, including climate modeling, protein structure prediction, and materials science ( Watson et al. (2023) ; Zeni et al. (2025) ; Li et al. (2024) ).
At their core, diffusion models are built upon two stochastic processes: a forward process that gradually corrupts the data distribution into pure noise, and a reverse process that generates samples by learning the logarithmic gradient of the perturbed marginals, commonly referred to as the score function.
Despite their broad empirical success, diffusion models have been predominantly developed for continuous data.
Their extension to discrete domains, such as natural language, graph-structured data, and categorical labels, has long remained challenging, although already discussed in Sohl-Dickstein et al. (2015) .
This perspective began to shift following the seminal work of Austin et al. (2021) , which revealed the promise of diffusion-based approaches in discrete settings.
Analogous to the continuous case, discrete diffusion models rely on a pair of noisy forward and reverse processes, with sampling achieved by learning appropriate ratios of distributions. Among recent developments ( Campbell et al. (2022) ; Ou et al. (2025) ; Sahoo et al. (2024) ; Bach and Saremi (2025) ), score-entropy discrete diffusion (SEDD) has demonstrated striking performance in text generation ( Lou et al. (2024) ), challenging the long-standing dominance of autoregressive language models. In contrast to autoregressive approaches, diffusion-based language models are not constrained to a fixed generation order (such as left-to-right), and they naturally lend themselves to more flexible forms of controlled generation, including conditional and structured text synthesis.
The promise of discrete diffusion models has spurred growing interest in their theoretical foundations. A particularly influential line of work formulates discrete diffusion through the lens of continuous-time Markov chains (CTMCs) ( Campbell et al., 2022 ) , in which the forward dynamics is governed by a carefully designed rate matrix, and the backward dynamics is approximated via a learned score function.
Among the proposed constructions, two choices have emerged as especially prominent: the uniform rate matrix, which induces a uniform stationary distribution for the forward process, and the absorbing rate matrix, which yields a degenerate stationary distribution with an absorbing state. In practice, the performance of the resulting samplers depends sensitively on the choice of the rate matrix ( Lou et al. (2024) ; von Rütte et al. (2025) ).
Correspondingly, two parallel lines of work have sought to understand the sampling efficiency of discrete diffusion models — specifically, the number of steps required to produce sufficiently accurate samples — under these respective constructions. Representative results include Chen and Ying (2025) ; Ren et al. (2025) ; Zhang et al. (2025) ; Pham et al. (2025) ; Liang et al. (2025c) for uniform diffusion and Park et al. (2025) ; Liang et al. (2025b) ; Conforti et al. (2025) ; Li and Cai (2025) ; Chen et al. (2025) for masking diffusion (also referred to as absorbing diffusion).
Existing theoretical analyses for score-based discrete diffusions suggest that convergence rates typically scale at least linearly with both the vocabulary size S S and the ambient dimension d d . Such scaling can quickly become prohibitive in applications; for instance, in GPT-2-based tasks, the vocabulary size is S = 50,257 S=50{,}257 and the dimension is d = 10 2 ∼ 10 3 d=10^{2}\sim 10^{3} ( Lou et al., 2024 ) . These considerations naturally motivate a fundamental question:
How efficient are discrete diffusion models? When is sublinear convergence possible?
1.1 Sampling efficiency and adaptivity
To put our discussion in context, there has been substantial progress in understanding the sample efficiency of continuous diffusion models. Seminal work by Chen et al. (2023b) characterizes the iteration complexity of the DDPM sampler under Lipschitz (or smoothness) assumptions on the score functions across all steps. Subsequent studies significantly relax these conditions and establish convergence guarantees for broader classes of continuous distributions ( Benton et al., 2024 ; Li et al., 2023 ; Chen et al., 2023a ) . Nevertheless, it is now well understood that for general distributions, a linear dependence on the ambient dimension d d is unavoidable. By contrast, when the target distribution exhibits additional structure — such as Gaussian mixture models or support on low-dimensional manifolds — a growing body of work shows that popular samplers can adaptively exploit intrinsic low-dimensional geometry, achieving improved efficiency without explicit dimension reduction (see, e.g., Li and Yan (2024) ; Li et al. (2025) ; Huang et al. (2024) ; Liang et al. (2025a) ).
The landscape shifts considerably as we move to discrete diffusion models. Under the CTMC formulation, algorithms such as Gillespie’s method and uniformization allow for exact simulation of the reverse process, free of discretization error ( Gillespie, 1976 ; Van Dijk, 1992 ; Chen and Ying, 2025 ) . However, these methods suffer from high computational costs in high-dimensional settings.
Moreover, their convergence guarantees are inherently stochastic, as they depend on a random number of transitions. An alternative and widely adopted approach, particularly in diffusion-based language models, is provided by τ \tau -leaping and its variants, including truncated τ \tau -leaping ( Gillespie, 2001 ; Campbell et al., 2022 ) . Originally developed in chemical kinetics, τ \tau -leaping replaces sequential state transitions with parallel updates across coordinates, offering substantial computational gains in large systems.
Yet, our theoretical understanding of τ \tau -leaping remains incomplete. Current state-of-the-art results exhibit at least linear dependence on the vocabulary size S S , linear dependence on d d for the absorbing case, and quadratic dependence on d d for the uniform case ( Liang et al. (2025b) ; Liang et al. (2025c) ; Conforti et al. (2025) ); see Table 1 for more details. It remains an open question whether these dependencies are fundamental information-theoretic barriers or merely analytical artifacts. Furthermore, as in the continuous setting, an ideal sampling algorithm should automatically adjust to the intrinsic difficulty of the target distribution. For example, one would expect substantially faster convergence for Dirac delta measures or uniform target distributions, without prior knowledge of the structure or modifications to the algorithm. Existing analyses of
τ \tau -leaping do not illuminate whether such adaptivity is possible.
More specifically, we aim to address the following question:
Can score-based samplers automatically adapt to structured target distributions?
1.2 Our contributions
The contributions of this work are centered on establishing sharp convergence guarantees for discrete diffusion models, bridging the gap between empirical success and theoretical understanding. Specifically, our contributions are mainly threefold:
•
Optimal rates for uniform diffusion : We establish that for the uniform diffusion process, the τ \tau -leaping sampler requires only O ~ ​ ( d / ε ) \widetilde{O}(d/\varepsilon) discretization steps to achieve an ε \varepsilon -error in KL divergence. This result significantly sharpens the previously best-known bound of O ~ ​ ( d 2 ​ S / ε ) \widetilde{O}(d^{2}S/\varepsilon) ( Liang et al., 2025c ) , effectively removing a factor of d d and the dependence on the vocabulary size S S .
•
Fundamental lower bounds : We demonstrate that the linear dependence on the dimension d d is essentially unimprovable for the τ \tau -leaping algorithm. Specifically, we show that under uniform diffusion, an o ⁡ ( d ) o(d) complexity bound is unattainable unless the target distribution is already proximal to the uniform measure. This result characterizes a fundamental price of sampling for informative distributions.
•
Adaptivity for masking diffusion : For the masking diffusion process, we introduce a refined τ \tau -leaping sampler that has a complexity governed by O ~ ​ ( 𝒟 / ε ) \widetilde{O}(\mathcal{D}/\varepsilon) , where 𝒟 \mathcal{D} is the effective total correlation, an information-theoretic measure of the target distribution’s intrinsic complexity. Notably, while 𝒟 \mathcal{D} is always bounded by the classical total correlation and the dual total correlation (and thus by d ​ log ⁡ S d\log S ), it can be sublinear or even O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) for highly structured data, allowing our sampler to adapt automatically to low-dimensional target distributions.
In contrast to prior work, our upper bounds
do not require the boundedness of the score estimator or any auxiliary regularity assumptions beyond control of the score entropy loss. The key technical ingredients include a Girsanov change-of-measure argument, combined with establishing the martingale properties of the sampling dynamics. The latter effectively separates the approximation error from the discretization error, allowing each to be analyzed independently.
For the lower bound, we leverage a log-Sobolev inequality together with a strong data-processing inequality along the uniform noising process.
To demonstrate the scope of our adaptivity results for masking discrete diffusion, we present several examples whose analysis requires careful constructions and delicate control of information-theoretic quantities, which may be of independent interest.
1.3 Notation
For a positive integer n n , we define [ n ] ≔ { 1 , … , n } [n]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,n\} and let I n ∈ ℝ n × n I_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n} denote the identity matrix.
Let d > 0 d>0 denote the number of dimensions, S > 0 S>0 denote the vocabulary size and T > 0 T>0 denote the time horizon.
Let MASK \mathrm{MASK} denote a special value outside of [ S ] [S] . Let 𝒳 ≔ 𝒱 d {\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq{\mathcal{V}}^{d} denote the domain, where, depending on the context, 𝒱 ≔ [ S ] {\mathcal{V}}\coloneqq[S] or 𝒱 ≔ [ S ] ∪ { MASK } {\mathcal{V}}\coloneqq[S]\cup\{\mathrm{MASK}\} .
We denote the set of all distributions on 𝒳 {\mathcal{X}} by 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) {\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{X}}) . Let ℋ \mathcal{H} , 𝖪𝖫 \mathsf{KL} , and I \mathrm{I} denote entropy , Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence , and mutual information , respectively. Let δ x \delta_{x} denote the Dirac measure at point x x . We adopt the standard asymptotic notation O ⁡ ( ⋅ ) , Ω ⁡ ( ⋅ ) , Θ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) O(\cdot),{\Omega}(\cdot),\Theta(\cdot) , ≲ \lesssim , and ≪ \ll . Additionally, O ~ ​ ( ⋅ ) , Ω ~ ​ ( ⋅ ) \widetilde{O}(\cdot),\widetilde{\Omega}(\cdot) , and Θ ~ ​ ( ⋅ ) \widetilde{\Theta}(\cdot) are defined analogously, except that the logarithmic dependency on d , S d,S , 1 / ε 1/\varepsilon , and 1 / δ 1/\delta is hidden. 2 Preliminaries of discrete diffusion models
2.1 A continuous-time Markov chain formulation
Our goal is to model d d -dimensional discrete data X 0 = ( X 0 1 , X 0 2 , … , X 0 d ) ∈ [ S ] d X_{0}=(X_{0}^{1},X_{0}^{2},\ldots,X_{0}^{d})\in[S]^{d} .
Let q data = q 0 q_{\mathrm{data}}=q_{0} denote the probability mass function (p.m.f.) of X 0 X_{0} from which we aim to sample, and let q 0 i q_{0}^{i} be the marginal p.m.f. of the i i -th coordinate.
Analogous to continuous diffusion models, their discrete counterparts consist of a forward and a reverse process over the discrete space.
The forward process.
We define a forward noising process that progressively transforms the data distribution q 0 q_{0} to a distribution q T q_{T} that is close to an easy-to-sample distribution. This process is modeled using a continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC).
Definition 1 (Continuous-time Markov chain) .
A CTMC with an initial distribution q 0 q_{0} and rate matrices ( Q t ) t ∈ [ 0 , T ] (Q_{t})_{t\in[0,T]} is a right-continuous stochastic process ( x t ) t ∈ [ 0 , T ] (x_{t})_{t\in[0,T]} such that
•
( x t ) t ∈ [ 0 , T ] (x_{t})_{t\in[0,T]} satisfies the Markov property : for any 0 ≤ s < t ≤ T 0\leq s<t\leq T , the conditional distribution of x t x_{t} given the history { x u , u ≤ s } \{x_{u},u\leq s\} depends only on x s x_{s} ,
•
for any 0 ≤ t < T 0\leq t<T , the transition probabilities satisfy, as Δ ​ t → 0 + \Delta t\to 0^{+} :
Pr ( x t + Δ ​ t = y ∣ x t = x ) = 𝕀 { x = y } + Q t ( x , y ) Δ t + o ( Δ t ) . \Pr(x_{t+\Delta t}=y\mid x_{t}=x)={\mathbb{I}}\{x=y\}+Q_{t}(x,y)\Delta t+o(\Delta t).
(1)
Here, the rate matrices satisfy Q t ​ ( x , y ) ≥ 0 Q_{t}(x,y)\geq 0 for all x ≠ y ∈ 𝒳 x\neq y\in\mathcal{X} and Q t ( x , x ) = − ∑ y ≠ x Q t ( x , y ) Q_{t}(x,x)=-\sum_{y\neq x}Q_{t}(x,y) .
We refer to Feller (1940) ; Feinberg et al. (2014) for a rigorous treatment of CTMCs.
For a given q 0 q_{0} , the marginals ( q t ) t ∈ [ 0 , T ] (q_{t})_{t\in[0,T]} satisfying Equation 1 are the solutions to the Kolmogorov forward equation :
d ​ q t d ​ t = Q t ⊤ ​ q t , for ​ 0 ≤ t ≤ T . \frac{\mathrm{d}q_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}=Q_{t}^{\top}q_{t},\quad\text{ for }0\leq t\leq T.
The reverse process.
For such a CTMC, there exists a time-reversed process with an initial distribution q T q_{T} , rate matrices ( Q ← t ) t ∈ [ 0 , T ] (\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]} , and marginals ( q ← t ) t ∈ [ 0 , T ] (\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]} , such that q t ≡ q ← T − t q_{t}\equiv\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{T-t} , for t ∈ [ 0 , T ] t\in[0,T] .
The forward and reverse rate matrices are explicitly related ( Campbell et al., 2022 ) by
Q ← t ​ ( x , y ) = Q T − t ​ ( y , x ) ​ q T − t ​ ( y ) q T − t ​ ( x ) , for ​ x ≠ y ∈ 𝒳 ​ and ​ 0 ≤ t ≤ T . \overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x,y)=Q_{T-t}(y,x)\frac{q_{T-t}(y)}{q_{T-t}(x)},\quad\text{ for }x\neq y\in{\mathcal{X}}\text{ and }0\leq t\leq T.
(2)
In this paper, we focus on rate matrices that satisfy three conditions:
1.
they are time-homogeneous, Q t ≡ Q Q_{t}\equiv Q ,
2.
Q ⁡ ( x , y ) = 0 Q(x,y)=0 whenever d_H ⁡ ( x , y ) ≥ 2 \ham(x,y)\geq 2 ,
3.
if d_H ⁡ ( x , y ) = 1 \ham(x,y)=1 and x i ≠ y i x^{i}\neq y^{i} , then Q ⁡ ( x , y ) = Q tok ​ ( x i , y i ) Q(x,y)=Q^{\mathrm{tok}}(x^{i},y^{i}) , for some fixed matrix Q tok Q^{\mathrm{tok}} .
In particular, we consider two important instances of CTMCs that are widely adopted in practice, namely the uniform noising process and the masking (or absorbing) noising process , which are defined through the choice of Q tok Q^{\mathrm{tok}} .
•
uniform noising process : A CTMC is a uniform noising process if for a ≠ b ∈ [ S ] a\neq b\in[S]
Q tok ​ ( a , b ) = 1 / S . \displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{tok}}(a,b)=1/S.
(3)
This CTMC converges to the uniform distribution on the domain 𝒳 ≔ [ S ] d {\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq[S]^{d} in the limit.
•
masking noising process :
A CTMC on the domain 𝒳 ≔ ( [ S ] ∪ { MASK } ) d {\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq([S]\cup\{\mathrm{MASK}\})^{d} is a masking noising process if for a ≠ b ∈ [ S ] ∪ { MASK } a\neq b\in[S]\cup\{\mathrm{MASK}\}
Q tok ( a , b ) = 𝕀 { a ≠ MASK and b = MASK } . \displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{tok}}(a,b)={\mathbb{I}}\{a\neq\mathrm{MASK}\text{ and }b=\mathrm{MASK}\}.
(4)
The corresponding CTMC converges to the Dirac measure ( δ MASK ) ⊗ d (\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}})^{\otimes d} as t → ∞ t\to\infty .
Note that we constrain the initial distribution q 0 q_{0} to be supported on non-masked data, i.e., on [ S ] d [S]^{d} .
2.2 Score estimation
Recall that the reverse process is a CTMC with rate matrices satisfying the relation ( 2 ), which is similar to the reverse process in the continuous case. The density ratio here generalizes the typical score function ∇ x ​ log ​ q t ​ ( x ) \nabla_{x}\log q_{t}(x) in the continuous case and is often referred to as the (concrete) score function for discrete diffusion models ( Meng et al., 2022 ) . Formally, we define the score function s t ​ ( y , x ) s_{t}(y,x) as
s t ​ ( y , x ) = q t ​ ( y ) q t ​ ( x ) , for ​ x ≠ y ∈ 𝒳 . s_{t}(y,x)=\frac{q_{t}(y)}{q_{t}(x)},\quad\text{for }x\neq y\in{\mathcal{X}}.
Score entropy loss.
For both the uniform and masking noising processes,
the marginals ( q t ) (q_{t}) , and consequently the score function, are intractable in general. In practice, one therefore resorts to an approximation s ^ t ​ ( y , x ) \widehat{s}_{t}(y,x) of the true score function s t ​ ( y , x ) s_{t}(y,x) , which is learned from data sampled from the target distribution q 0 q_{0} .
To evaluate the quality of the estimated score, a widely used loss function is the score entropy loss , originally introduced in Lou et al. (2024) , which has since become the de facto standard for training score-based discrete diffusion models. This loss provides a principled objective for matching the approximate score s ^ t \widehat{s}_{t} to the true score induced by the forward diffusion process.
Specifically, for t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 and functions s ^ , s : 𝒳 × 𝒳 → ℝ + \widehat{s},s:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+} , the score entropy loss ℒ SE {\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SE}} is defined as follows:
ℒ SE ​ ( t , s ^ , s ) \displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SE}}(t,\widehat{s},s)
≔ 𝔼 x ∼ q t ​ [ ∑ y ≠ x Q t ​ ( y , x ) ​ s ​ ( y , x ) ​ D ​ ( s ^ ​ ( y , x ) , s ⁡ ( y , x ) ) ] ≥ 0 . \displaystyle\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim q_{t}}\Big[\sum_{y\neq x}Q_{t}(y,x)s(y,x)D(\widehat{s}(y,x),s(y,x))\Big]\geq 0.
Here, for
a , b ≥ 0 a,b\geq 0 , the Bregman divergence for ϕ ⁡ ( a ) = a − log ⁡ a \phi(a)=a-\log a is given by
D ⁡ ( a , b ) ≔ a b − 1 − log ⁡ a b ≥ 0 . D(a,b)\coloneqq\frac{a}{b}-1-\log\frac{a}{b}\geq 0.
In practice, to implement any sampling algorithm, one has to discretize the continuous dynamics and obtain score estimates at discrete time steps.
Suppose that the score estimates s ^ T − t \widehat{s}_{T-t} are obtained at discrete time points 0 ≤ t 0 < t 1 < … < t N ≤ T 0\leq t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{N}\leq T .
We make the following standard assumption regarding the score estimation errors.
Assumption 1 (Approximation error) .
Let N > 0 N>0 and 0 ≤ t 0 < t 1 < … < t N ≤ T 0\leq t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{N}\leq T . We assume that
∑ k = 0 N − 1 ( t k + 1 − t k ) ​ ℒ SE ​ ( T − t k , s ^ T − t k , s T − t k ) ≤ ε score . \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(t_{k+1}-t_{k}){\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SE}}(T-t_{k},\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}},s_{T-t_{k}})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{score}}.
(5)
This assumption is concerned with the aggregated estimation errors over all N N steps.
Several works have constructed estimates that satisfy this assumption; examples include Lou et al. (2024) ; Ou et al. (2025) ; Benton et al. (2024) .
2.3 Score-based sampling algorithms
Armed with the score estimates ( s ^ T − t ) t ∈ { t 0 , … , t N } (\widehat{s}_{T-t})_{t\in\{t_{0},\ldots,t_{N}\}} ,
we aim to construct a generative model q ^ 0 \widehat{q}_{0} that approximates the data distribution q 0 q_{0} . A natural approach proposed in Campbell et al. (2022) is to define a surrogate CTMC that starts from an easy-to-sample distribution p 0 ≈ q T p_{0}\approx q_{T} and approximates the backward dynamics in ( 2 ). Concretely, we define the time-inhomogeneous rate matrix
Q ^ t ​ ( x , y ) = Q T − t ​ ( y , x ) ​ s ^ T − t ​ ( y , x ) . \widehat{Q}_{t}(x,y)=Q_{T-t}(y,x)\widehat{s}_{T-t}(y,x).
(6)
In practice, score estimates are only available on a fixed discretization τ = ( t 0 , … , t N ) \tau=(t_{0},\ldots,t_{N}) , and extending these estimates to the full interval [ 0 , T ] [0,T] introduces
discretization error .
τ \tau -leaping algorithm.
As mentioned above, a widely adopted sampler is the τ \tau -leaping algorithm ( Campbell et al., 2022 ) , which approximates Equation 6
with multiple possible transitions within each discretization interval.
Formally, for k ∈ { 0 , … , N − 1 } k\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\} and t ∈ [ t k , t k + 1 ) t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}) , given x t k x_{t_{k}} and s ^ T − t k \widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}} , τ \tau -leaping obtains x t k + 1 x_{t_{k+1}} as a random vector whose coordinates are sampled independently via d d one-dimensional CTMCs. For each i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , the initial distribution is δ x t k i \delta_{x_{t_{k}}^{i}} and the rate matrices are given by
2 2
2
The algorithm admits an equivalent Poisson formulation, in which d ​ S dS Poisson random variables corresponding to coordinate-value transitions are sampled and applied in parallel. :
Q ^ t i ( a , b ) = s ^ T − t k ( x t k , x t k ⊕ i ( b − a ) ) , for a ≠ b ∈ 𝒱 . \widehat{Q}_{t}^{i}(a,b)=\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}}(x_{t_{k}},x_{t_{k}}\oplus_{i}(b-a)),\qquad\text{ for }a\neq b\in{\mathcal{V}}.
(7)
The formulation in Equation 7 requires either an additive structure on the state space or the restriction that each coordinate undergoes at most one transition between discretization points. Existing analyses for uniform and masking diffusions ( Campbell et al., 2022 ; Liang et al., 2025c ) adopt the latter assumption. In Section 3.1 , we explore the necessity of this requirement for the uniform noising process.
Lou et al. (2024) generalizes τ \tau -leaping by introducing a class of samplers termed τ \tau -leaping strategies , which allow arbitrary transformations x t k + 1 i = f k i ​ ( s ^ T − t k , x t k ) x_{t_{k+1}}^{i}=f^{i}_{k}(\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}},x_{t_{k}}) . Both the Euler method and Tweedie τ \tau -leaping fall into this class. However, they remain challenging for direct theoretical analysis due to the absence of a CTMC structure.
parallel updates CTMC-based Euler method Tweedie τ \tau -leaping τ \tau -leaping alg. Truncated τ \tau -leaping Algorithm 1 τ \tau -bridging Gillespie’s alg. Uniformization DMPM
Figure 1: Overview of score-based samplers. The left part comprises score-based samplers that allow parallel updates, defined as τ \tau -leaping strategies in Lou et al. (2024) . The right part consists of samplers that can be defined through the CTMC framework. At the intersection are τ \tau -bridging strategies, defined in Equation 8 .
This paper: τ \tau -bridging strategies.
We introduce a structured class of samplers that generalizes the τ \tau -leaping algorithm. We name this class of algorithms τ \tau -bridging strategies , which retain the parallel updating structure while remaining analytically tractable.
A τ \tau -bridging strategy generates x t k + 1 x_{t_{k+1}} from x t k x_{t_{k}} by evolving d d independent one-dimensional CTMCs on [ t k , t k + 1 ) [t_{k},t_{k+1}) . For each coordinate i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , the chain is initialized at δ x t k i \delta_{x_{t_{k}}^{i}} and has the rate matrix given by
Q ^ t i = G t i ​ ( s ^ T − t k , x t k ) , \widehat{Q}_{t}^{i}=G_{t}^{i}(\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}},x_{t_{k}}),
(8)
for some mapping G t i : ℝ + 𝒳 × 𝒳 × 𝒳 → ℝ 𝒱 × 𝒱 G_{t}^{i}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}} .
Compared to general τ \tau -leaping strategies, τ \tau -bridging strategies restrict updates to CTMC-based transitions. This restriction preserves parallel coordinate updates while facilitating theoretical analysis. Figure 1 summarizes the relationships between these classes of sampling algorithms.
A representative instance of a τ \tau -bridging sampler is the truncated τ \tau -leaping sampler of Liang et al. (2025c) .
For k ∈ [ N ] k\in[N] and t ∈ [ t k , t k + 1 ) t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}) , the corresponding rate matrices take the form:
G t i ( s ^ T − t k , x t k ) ( a , b ) = s ^ T − t k ( x t k , x t k ⊙ i b ) 𝕀 { x t k i = a } for a ≠ b ∈ 𝒱 . \displaystyle G_{t}^{i}(\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}},x_{t_{k}})(a,b)=\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}}(x_{t_{k}},x_{t_{k}}\odot_{i}b){\mathbb{I}}\{x_{t_{k}}^{i}=a\}\quad\text{for }a\neq b\in{\mathcal{V}}.
(9)
The indicator 𝕀 { x t k i = a } {\mathbb{I}}\{x_{t_{k}}^{i}=a\} enforces the constraint that at most one transition occurs per coordinate i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] within each discretization interval [ t k , t k + 1 ) [t_{k},t_{k+1}) . In Section 3.2 ,
we show that an instance of this scheme achieves sublinear complexity for the masking noising process under mild distributional assumptions. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first result establishing such a guarantee.
3 Main results
In this section, we characterize the sampling efficiency of the τ \tau -bridging strategies for both the uniform and masking noising processes.
We develop sharp convergence guarantees and highlight cases where adaptivity is automatically achieved. We provide proof sketches for all results in this section, with full proofs deferred to the appendix.
3.1 Uniform discrete diffusion
3.1.1 A sharp convergence characterization
We begin with the uniform discrete diffusion models, whose forward dynamics are given by the uniform noising process. We establish explicit sampling guarantees for the τ \tau -leaping algorithm, measured in KL divergence. The proof is given in Appendix C.1 .
Theorem 1 .
Let q data = q 0 q_{\mathrm{data}}=q_{0} be the data distribution on 𝒳 ≔ [ S ] d {\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq[S]^{d} . For 0 = t 0 < t 1 < … < t N = T 0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{N}=T , let Δ ≔ max k ⁡ { t k + 1 − t k } = O ⁡ ( 1 ) \Delta\coloneqq\max_{k}\{t_{k+1}-t_{k}\}=O(1) . Set p 0 = Unif ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) p_{0}=\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{X}}) .
Under Assumption 1 , the τ \tau -leaping algorithm initialized at p 0 p_{0} generates a sample from p output = p T p_{\mathrm{output}}=p_{T} such that
𝖪𝖫 ( q data ∥ p output ) ≲ ε score + e − T d log ( S ) + Δ d log ( S / Δ ) . \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(q_{\mathrm{data}}\,\|\,p_{\mathrm{output}})\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{score}}+e^{-T}d\log(S)+\Delta d\log(S/\Delta).
(10)
As expected, the KL divergence bound in Theorem 1 decomposes into three terms.
The first term ε score \varepsilon_{\mathrm{score}} quantifies the quality of score estimation and captures the accumulation of estimation errors over the N N discretization steps.
The second term corresponds to the initialization error, arising from initializing the sampler with the uniform distribution p 0 p_{0} instead of the true terminal distribution q T q_{T} ; this term decays exponentially with the diffusion horizon T T .
Finally, the third term accounts for the discretization error incurred by approximating the continuous-time reverse process with a discrete-time τ \tau -leaping scheme.
To further interpret Theorem 1 and place it in context with existing results, we highlight several of its salient features.
First, the discretization error scales linearly with the dimension d d and only logarithmically with the vocabulary size S S .
This matches the result obtained for the random walk model ( Conforti et al., 2025 ) and reveals that the discretization error is insensitive to the distribution scale, as has been shown for continuous diffusion models (e.g., Huang et al. (2024) ).
Second, the theorem permits a flexible choice of step size schedules and does not require early stopping. In contrast to prior analyses that rely on carefully selected step sizes and introduce an early stopping time δ \delta (where the algorithm outputs p T − δ p_{T-\delta} in place of p T p_{T} ), the bound in Theorem 1 depends only on the maximum step size.
Moreover, the same bound applies uniformly to early stopping variants: the right-hand side of ( 10 ) remains unchanged for any δ ≪ 1 \delta\ll 1 .
The only requirement we have on score estimation is Assumption 1 , with no additional boundedness or regularity conditions (typically assumed in the existing literature). As a result, the theorem applies to a broad class of score estimation procedures commonly used in practice.
We provide a sketch of its proof to illustrate the main proof ideas.
Proof sketch of Theorem 1 .
In view of the data-processing inequality and the chain rule for KL divergence, we upper bound the KL divergence between q 0 q_{0} and p T p_{T} by the KL divergence between the paths q T − t 0 , … , T − t N q_{T-t_{0},\ldots,T-t_{N}} and p t 0 , … , t N p_{t_{0},\ldots,t_{N}} , which can be decomposed as
𝖪𝖫 ( q 0 ∥ p T ) \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(q_{0}\,\|\,p_{T})
≤ 𝖪𝖫 ( q T − t 0 , … , T − t N ∥ p t 0 , … , t N ) \displaystyle\leq\mathsf{KL}(q_{T-t_{0},\ldots,T-t_{N}}\,\|\,p_{t_{0},\ldots,t_{N}})
= 𝖪𝖫 ( q T ∥ p 0 ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 𝔼 x t k ∼ q ← t k [ 𝖪𝖫 ( q ← t k + 1 | t k ( ⋅ | x t k ) ∥ p t k + 1 | t k ( ⋅ | x t k ) ) ] . \displaystyle=\mathsf{KL}(q_{T}\,\|\,p_{0})+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{t_{k}}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t_{k}}}\left[\mathsf{KL}\left(\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t_{k+1}\mid t_{k}}(\cdot|x_{t_{k}})\,\|\,p_{t_{k+1}\mid t_{k}}(\cdot|x_{t_{k}})\right)\right].
The first term is the initialization error, which can be upper bounded by the log-Sobolev inequality in Lemma 7 .
For the second term, we apply Girsanov’s change-of-measure theorem for continuous-time Markov chains to obtain the following upper bound:

The first term can be controlled by Assumption 1 after taking the expectation over x ℓ ∼ q ← ℓ x_{\ell}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{\ell} and integrating over time.
The second term can be shown to be zero with the help of Lemma 8 after taking the expectation over x t ∼ q ← t | ℓ ( ⋅ ∣ x ℓ ) x_{t}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid\ell}(\cdot\mid x_{\ell}) .
Thus, the problem boils down to upper bounding the third term above, whose properties are characterized in Lemma 10 .
After taking the expectation and integrating over time, we can upper bound the third term by Δ ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( S / Δ ) \Delta d\log(S/\Delta) .
Combining the bounds for all three terms completes the proof.
∎
Next, we specialize Theorem 1 to the concrete choice of a discretization schedule to derive the iteration complexity required to obtain an ε \varepsilon -accurate sampler in KL divergence.
For a simple step size schedule, it turns out that d / ε d/\varepsilon steps (up to logarithmic factors) suffice for convergence, significantly improving on the state-of-the-art complexity of d 2 ​ S / ε d^{2}S/\varepsilon from Liang et al. (2025c) . Refer to Appendix C.2 for the proof.
Corollary 1 .
For the setting in Theorem 1 and ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , the output of the τ \tau -leaping algorithm with constant step size schedule t k + 1 − t k = T / N t_{k+1}-t_{k}=T/N for k ∈ [ N − 1 ] k\in[N-1] , achieves
𝖪𝖫 ( q data ∥ p output ) ≲ ε score + ε , \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(q_{\mathrm{data}}\,\|\,p_{\mathrm{output}})\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{score}}+\varepsilon,
provided that the time horizon T = log ⁡ ( d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) / ε ) T=\log(d\log(S)/\varepsilon) and the iteration number
N = O ~ ​ ( d ε ) . \displaystyle N=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right).
(11)
Remark 1 (Step size schedule) .
In Corollary 1 , we adopt the constant step size schedule for simplicity. This choice is optimal in the sense that it minimizes the worst-case upper bound for a fixed number of steps N N , and it is also empirically effective ( Campbell et al., 2022 ) .
However, other step size schedules commonly used in practice and theory achieve the same iteration complexity, including the exponential-then-constant schedule (defined as in Corollary 2 and used in Liang et al. (2025c) ) and the log-linear schedule ( Lou et al., 2024 ) .
In these works, early stopping is introduced to maintain numerical stability in score estimation during training and also to ensure a small discretization error. Our result shows that, under Assumption 1 , early stopping is not necessary for a small discretization error.
3.1.2 A matching lower bound for τ \tau -leaping
While Theorem 1 establishes an upper bound for the τ \tau -leaping algorithm scaling nearly linearly with the dimension d d and logarithmically with the vocabulary size S S , the fundamental question remains: is this dependence an intrinsic limit or merely a technical artifact? We show that the former is indeed the case
by establishing a matching lower bound.
We note that for target distributions sufficiently close to the uniform distribution, sampling can be achieved with very few steps, as the forward CTMC converges efficiently to its limit.
To avoid these pathological instances,
we restrict our focus to the class of distributions that remain sufficiently well-separated from the uniform distribution.
Specifically, for any γ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \gamma\in[0,1] , define the subset 𝒫 γ ​ ( 𝒳 ) ⊆ 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) {\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma}({\mathcal{X}})\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{X}}) as
𝒫 γ ​ ( 𝒳 ) = { q 0 ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) : ℋ ⁡ ( q 1 ) ≤ ( 1 − γ ) ⋅ ℋ ⁡ ( Unif ​ ( 𝒳 ) ) = ( 1 − γ ) ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) } , \displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma}({\mathcal{X}})=\big\{q_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{X}}):\mathcal{H}(q_{1})\leq(1-\gamma)\cdot\mathcal{H}(\text{Unif}({\mathcal{X}}))=(1-\gamma)d\log(S)\big\},
where q 1 q_{1} is the marginal distribution at t = 1 t=1 of the uniform noising process initialized at q 0 q_{0} , Unif ​ ( 𝒳 ) \text{Unif}({\mathcal{X}}) is the uniform distribution on 𝒳 {\mathcal{X}} , and ℋ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathcal{H}(\cdot) denotes the entropy function of a distribution.
Intuitively, for γ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \gamma\in(0,1) , the class 𝒫 γ ​ ( 𝒳 ) {\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma}({\mathcal{X}}) imposes a structural constraint on the convergence of the forward process; it describes distributions that do not mix rapidly.
In this sense, for γ = O ⁡ ( 1 ) \gamma=O(1) , 𝒫 γ ​ ( 𝒳 ) {\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma}({\mathcal{X}}) contains distributions that remain informative enough at time t = 1 t=1 in the forward process. This covers most distributions of practical interest, since they carry nontrivial information characterized by relatively low entropy.
The following lower bound shows that, when sampling from a distribution in 𝒫 γ ​ ( 𝒳 ) {\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma}({\mathcal{X}}) with the τ \tau -leaping algorithm, the iteration complexity bound in Corollary 1 cannot be improved up to logarithmic factors.
The proof is given in Appendix C.3 .
Theorem 2 .
For any target distribution q 0 ∈ 𝒫 γ ​ ( 𝒳 ) q_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma}({\mathcal{X}}) and early stopping time 0 ≤ δ ≪ 1 0\leq\delta\ll 1 , denote the path measure of the backward process by Q ​ = 𝑑 ​ { q ← t } t ∈ [ 0 , T − δ ] Q\overset{d}{=}\{\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T-\delta]} and the sampling process by P ​ = 𝑑 ​ { p t } t ∈ [ 0 , T − δ ] P\overset{d}{=}\{p_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T-\delta]} . Let γ = Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) \gamma=\Omega(1) . Then, for any step size schedule 0 = t 0 < t 1 < … < t N = T − δ 0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{N}=T-\delta with max k ⁡ { t k + 1 − t k } ≤ 1 2 \max_{k}\{t_{k+1}-t_{k}\}\leq\frac{1}{2} , it takes the τ \tau -leaping algorithm at least
N = Ω ⁡ ( d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) ) \displaystyle N={\Omega}(d\log(S))
(12)
iterations to achieve
𝖪𝖫 ( Q ∥ P ) ≤ ε score + O ( 1 ) . \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(Q\,\|\,P)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{score}}+O(1).
We make several remarks concerning the nature and implications of our lower bound.
Theorem 2 reveals that for informative target distributions in 𝒫 γ ​ ( 𝒳 ) \mathcal{P}^{\gamma}(\mathcal{X}) , ensuring that the KL divergence between the sampling process and the reverse process is small requires the number of steps to scale at least linearly with the dimension d d , which cannot be avoided for general distributions.
In addition, the lower bound is uniform over both early stopping schedules ( 0 < δ ≪ 1 0<\delta\ll 1 ) and non-early stopping schemes ( δ = 0 \delta=0 ).
This lower bound is algorithm-dependent: it relies on structural properties of the τ \tau -leaping algorithm and therefore differs fundamentally from information-theoretic or minimax lower bounds.
In principle, alternative sampling schemes may circumvent the linear dependence on d d . Indeed, in Section 3.2 , we show that a modified τ \tau -leaping procedure achieves sublinear dependence on d d for structured target distributions under the masking noising process. Whether analogous improvements are possible for uniform discrete diffusion through modified algorithms remains an open question.
When the target distribution has high entropy,
the lower bound need not apply. Indeed, when q data q_{\mathrm{data}} satisfies 𝖪𝖫 ( q data ∥ Unif ( 𝒳 ) ) = o ( d ) \mathsf{KL}(q_{\mathrm{data}}\|\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{X}}))=o(d) , one can show that ℋ ⁡ ( q 1 ) = Θ ⁡ ( d ​ log ⁡ S ) \mathcal{H}(q_{1})=\Theta(d\log S) , and that a sample from a distribution with the KL error at most ε score + ε \varepsilon_{\mathrm{score}}+\varepsilon can be obtained using N = o ⁡ ( d ) N=o(d) steps. A precise formulation of this claim is given in Appendix C.4 .
We remark that the quantity controlled in Theorem 2 is the KL divergence between two path measures, rather than the divergence between the terminal output distributions, which may appear weaker than the upper bound in Corollary 1 . However, to the best of our knowledge, all existing upper-bound analyses for the KL divergence, including ours, proceed by first bounding the KL divergence between path measures and then invoking the data-processing inequality. Consequently, the lower bound applies to all current analysis techniques. In this sense, Theorem 2 establishes the optimality of the iteration complexity in Corollary 1 within the scope of the existing analysis techniques.
Finally, we provide the proof sketch of Theorem 2 to illustrate the main proof techniques.
Proof sketch of Theorem 2 .
The proof is based on a refined analysis of the decay of KL divergence along the forward process for any distribution q 0 ∈ 𝒫 γ ​ ( 𝒳 ) q_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma}({\mathcal{X}}) . While we state our result for γ = Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) \gamma=\Omega(1) , the proof works for every γ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) . \gamma\in(0,1).
It can be shown that the KL divergence along the forward process is a differentiable function of time t t , and we denote its negative rate of change as φ ⁡ ( t ) \varphi(t) , i.e.,
φ ( t ) = − d d ​ t 𝖪𝖫 ( q t ∥ p 0 ) = ∑ x , y : d_H ⁡ ( x , y ) = 1 q t ( x ) s t ( y , x ) log ( s t ​ ( y , x ) s t ​ ( x , y ) ) , \displaystyle\varphi(t)=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathsf{KL}(q_{t}\|p_{0})=\sum_{x,y:\ham(x,y)=1}q_{t}(x)s_{t}(y,x)\log\left(\frac{s_{t}(y,x)}{s_{t}(x,y)}\right),
where p 0 = Unif ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) p_{0}=\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{X}}) is the limit distribution of the forward noising process.

Since max k ⁡ { t k + 1 − t k } ≤ 1 2 \max_{k}\{t_{k+1}-t_{k}\}\leq\frac{1}{2} , we can choose a suitable M M , such that 1 < M < N 1<M<N and T − t M ∈ [ 1 2 , 1 ] T-t_{M}\in[\frac{1}{2},1] .
Combining this with Eqn. ( 14 ), we obtain
∑ k = M N − 1 ( t k + 1 − t k ) 2 ≲ 1 γ ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) , \displaystyle\sum_{k=M}^{N-1}(t_{k+1}-t_{k})^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\gamma d\log(S)},
which implies that N = Ω ⁡ ( γ ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) ) N=\Omega(\gamma d\log(S)) by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎
3.2 Masking discrete diffusion
We now turn our attention to the masking noising process.
Our main result in this setting is an upper bound that intrinsically depends on the structural properties of the target distribution q data q_{\mathrm{data}} ,
rather than scaling with the ambient dimension d d .
This aligns with the intuition that for highly structured distributions — such as a sparse mixture of Dirac measures — a sensible sampler should converge at a sublinear scale, or perhaps even logarithmically with d d .
3.2.1 Preliminaries
We begin by recalling two fundamental quantities in information theory: the total correlation and the dual total correlation.
For a distribution q q over [ S ] d [S]^{d} and x ∼ q x\sim q , the total correlation 𝒞 ⁡ ( q ) {\mathcal{C}}(q) and the dual total correlation ℬ ⁡ ( q ) {\mathcal{B}}(q) are defined as
𝒞 ⁡ ( q ) ≔ ∑ i = 1 d ℋ ⁡ ( x i ) − ℋ ⁡ ( x ) ​ and ​ ℬ ​ ( q ) ≔ ℋ ⁡ ( x ) − ∑ i = 1 d ℋ ⁡ ( x i ∣ x − i ) . {\mathcal{C}}(q)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{H}(x^{i})-\mathcal{H}(x)\text{ and }{\mathcal{B}}(q)\coloneqq\mathcal{H}(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{H}(x^{i}\mid x^{-i}).
(15)
We now introduce a time-dependent quantity associated with the masking noising process.
Consider a masking noising process defined by Equation 4 with marginals ( q t ) t ≥ 0 (q_{t})_{t\geq 0} . For x ∈ ( [ S ] ∪ { MASK } ) d x\in([S]\cup\{\mathrm{MASK}\})^{d} and i ≠ j ∈ [ d ] i\neq j\in[d] , let x − ( i , j ) x^{-(i,j)} denote the collection of all unmasked elements of x x , excluding the i i -th and the j j -th coordinates.
We define the effective total correlation of the target distribution as
𝒟 ⁡ ( q 0 ) ≔ ∫ 0 ∞ min ⁡ ( 1 , t ) ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t with ℐ ⁡ ( t ) ≔ ∑ i ≠ j ∈ [ d ] I ⁡ ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) ≥ 0 , \displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}(q_{0})\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\infty}\min(1,t){\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{with}\quad{\mathcal{I}}(t)\coloneqq\sum_{i\neq j\in[d]}\mathrm{I}(x_{t}^{i};x_{t}^{j}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})\geq 0,
(16)
where I ⁡ ( A ; B ∣ C ) \mathrm{I}(A;B\mid C) denotes the conditional mutual information, and x t ∼ q t x_{t}\sim q_{t} .
Lemma 16 shows that the total correlation and the dual total correlation can be expressed through ℐ ⁡ ( t ) {\mathcal{I}}(t) by
ℬ ⁡ ( q 0 ) = ∫ 0 ∞ ℐ ⁡ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t and 𝒞 ⁡ ( q 0 ) = ∫ 0 ∞ ( e t − 1 ) ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t . {\mathcal{B}}(q_{0})=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{C}}(q_{0})=\int_{0}^{\infty}(e^{t}-1){\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t.
Consequently, 𝒟 ⁡ ( q 0 ) ≤ min ⁡ ( ℬ ⁡ ( q 0 ) , 𝒞 ⁡ ( q 0 ) ) {\mathcal{D}}(q_{0})\leq\min({\mathcal{B}}(q_{0}),{\mathcal{C}}(q_{0})) .
The statement and the proof of this result are given in Appendix E.1 .
Note that both ℬ ⁡ ( q 0 ) , 𝒞 ⁡ ( q 0 ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{0}),{\mathcal{C}}(q_{0}) , and hence 𝒟 ⁡ ( q 0 ) {\mathcal{D}}(q_{0}) are upper bounded by d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) d\log(S) . Moreover, there exist distributions q 0 q_{0} with ℬ ⁡ ( q 0 ) = O ⁡ ( 1 ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{0})=O(1) while 𝒞 ⁡ ( q 0 ) = Ω ⁡ ( d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) ) {\mathcal{C}}(q_{0})=\Omega(d\log(S)) , and vice versa. We refer to Austin (2020) for a detailed study of the total correlation and the dual total correlation. Importantly, there are also natural distributions for which both ℬ ⁡ ( q 0 ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{0}) and 𝒞 ⁡ ( q 0 ) {\mathcal{C}}(q_{0}) are of order d d , while 𝒟 ⁡ ( q 0 ) {\mathcal{D}}(q_{0}) remains small. See Proposition 5 for an example of such a distribution.
3.2.2 An adaptive characterization

Equipped with the above preliminaries, we present our main result on the masking noising process. The proof is given in Appendix D.1 .
Theorem 3 .
Let q data = q 0 q_{\mathrm{data}}=q_{0} be the target distribution on [ S ] d [S]^{d} . For 0 = t 0 < t 1 < … < t N = T 0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{N}=T , let h k ≔ t k + 1 − t k h_{k}\coloneqq t_{k+1}-t_{k} be the step sizes and assume that Δ ≔ max k ⁡ h k = O ⁡ ( 1 ) \Delta\coloneqq\max_{k}h_{k}=O(1) . Let
p 0 ≔ ( ( 1 − e − T ) ​ δ MASK + S − 1 ​ e − T ​ ∑ k = 1 S δ k ) ⊗ d . \displaystyle p_{0}\coloneqq\left(\big(1-e^{-T}\big)\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}}+S^{-1}e^{-T}\sum_{k=1}^{S}\delta_{k}\right)^{\otimes d}.
Under Assumption 1 , Algorithm 1 initialized at p 0 p_{0}
produces a sample from p output = p T p_{\mathrm{output}}=p_{T} such that
𝖪𝖫 ( q data ∥ p output ) ≲ ε score + e − T d log ( S ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 h k ∫ T − t k + 1 T − t k ℐ ( t ) d t . \mathsf{KL}(q_{\mathrm{data}}\,\|\,p_{\mathrm{output}})\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{score}}+e^{-T}d\log(S)+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}h_{k}\int_{T-t_{k+1}}^{T-t_{k}}{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t.
(17)
A few remarks on the consequences and implications of Theorem 3 are in order.
As in Theorem 1 , the last term in the upper bound
corresponds to the discretization error measured using the integrated mutual information defined in Equation 16 .
While the first two terms are generic, the third term governs the dependence on the dimension d d and reflects the information-theoretic properties of the target distribution.
For structured distributions, our algorithm implicitly adapts to the underlying structure of the target distribution without requiring any prior knowledge of that structure or any modification to the algorithm itself.
In Appendix D.3 , we analyze the performance of truncated τ \tau -leaping as an alternative to Algorithm 1 , which has an additional d / N 2 {d}/{N^{2}} term in the upper bound Equation 17 , ignoring lower-order contributions. Although for structured target distributions the resulting iteration complexity already scales as d \sqrt{d} rather than d d (as in the existing literature), it does not fully adapt to the geometry of the target distribution.
To provide some intuition, the standard (or truncated) τ \tau -leaping algorithm informally satisfies for t ∈ [ t k , t k + 1 ) t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}) (see Equation 9 )
G t i ​ ( s T − t k , x t k ) ≈ G t k i ​ ( s T − t k , x t k ) , and thus Q ^ t ≈ Q ← t k , G_{t}^{i}(s_{T-t_{k}},x_{t_{k}})\approx G_{t_{k}}^{i}(s_{T-t_{k}},x_{t_{k}}),\quad\text{and thus}\quad\widehat{Q}_{t}\approx\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t_{k}},
(18)
where we recall the mapping G t i G_{t}^{i} from Equation 8 .
That is, even when the score estimation is exact, s ^ T − t k ≡ s T − t k \widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}}\equiv s_{T-t_{k}} , the τ \tau -leaping algorithm introduces a mismatch between the surrogate and true rate matrices as s T − t k ≢ s T − t s_{T-t_{k}}\nequiv s_{T-t} .
Algorithm 1 corrects this discrepancy by enforcing
G t i ​ ( s T − t k , x t k ) ≈ G t i ​ ( s T − t , x t k ) , and thus Q ^ t ≈ Q ← t , G_{t}^{i}(s_{T-t_{k}},x_{t_{k}})\approx G_{t}^{i}(s_{T-t},x_{t_{k}}),\quad\text{and thus}\quad\widehat{Q}_{t}\approx\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t},
(19)
through the rescaling of the score estimate function: s ^ T − t = e T − t k − 1 e T − t − 1 ​ s ^ T − t k \widehat{s}_{T-t}=\frac{e^{T-t_{k}}-1}{e^{T-t}-1}\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}} . As it is a linear transformation of the score estimate function, we can simulate its dynamics only at discrete points T − t 0 , … , T − t N T-t_{0},\ldots,T-t_{N} (see Algorithm 1 and Lemma 13 ).
This leads to a sharper upper bound in Theorem 3 relative to the analogous bound for truncated τ \tau -leaping (Theorem 5 ; see also Remark 3 ).
Empirically, the benefit of rescaling the score function in masking discrete diffusion models has also been observed in prior work; see, for example, Lou et al. (2024) ; Ou et al. (2025) .
Notably, our results are closely connected to an intriguing parallel line of work on the masking diffusion models ( Li and Cai (2025) ; Chen et al. (2025) ), which focuses on the design of unmasking schedules without adopting a CTMC perspective. In particular, Chen et al. (2025) derives optimal unmasking schedules and discusses two representative instances in which the number of steps scales linearly with ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) and 𝒞 ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{C}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) , respectively. Their algorithms require an a priori estimate of ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) and 𝒞 ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{C}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) or a doubling search procedure to calibrate the unmasking schedule and rely on a different sampling mechanism. The fact that our score-based sampler automatically exploits similar information-theoretic quantities without additional hyperparameters underscores both the fundamental nature of these quantities and the robustness of the CTMC framework.
Below we provide a proof sketch of Theorem 3 with the details deferred to Appendix D.1 .
Proof sketch of Theorem 3 .
First, Lemma 13 shows that Algorithm 1 outputs a sample from a CTMC with initial distribution p 0 p_{0} and rate matrices
Q ^ t ​ ( x , y ) ≔ { s ^ T − t k ( x t k ⊙ i y i , x t k ) e T − t k − 1 e T − t − 1 𝕀 { x i = MASK } , if d_H ( x , y ) = 1 , x i ≠ y i , and x t k i = MASK , − ∑ z ≠ x Q ^ t ( x , z ) , if ​ y = x , 0 , otherwise. \widehat{Q}_{t}(x,y)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}}(x_{t_{k}}\odot_{i}y^{i},x_{t_{k}})\frac{e^{T-t_{k}}-1}{e^{T-t}-1}{\mathbb{I}}\{x^{i}=\mathrm{MASK}\},&\quad\text{if }\ham(x,y)=1,x^{i}\neq y^{i},\text{ and }x_{t_{k}}^{i}=\mathrm{MASK},\\
-\sum_{z\neq x}\widehat{Q}_{t}(x,z),&\quad\text{if }y=x,\\
0,&\quad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}
(20)
This corresponds to a τ \tau -bridging strategy ( Equation 8 ) with the following function G t i ​ ( s ^ T − t k , x t k ) G^{i}_{t}(\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}},x_{t_{k}}) :
G t i ( s ^ T − t k , x t k ) ( a , b ) = e T − t k − 1 e T − t − 1 s ^ T − t k ( x t k , x t k ⊙ i b ) 𝕀 { x t k i = a } for a ≠ b ∈ 𝒱 . G^{i}_{t}(\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}},x_{t_{k}})(a,b)=\frac{e^{T-t_{k}}-1}{e^{T-t}-1}\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}}(x_{t_{k}},x_{t_{k}}\odot_{i}b){\mathbb{I}}\{x^{i}_{t_{k}}=a\}\quad\text{for }a\neq b\in{\mathcal{V}}.
By the data-processing inequality, we upper bound the KL divergence between q 0 q_{0} and p T p_{T} by the KL divergence between the paths q T − t 0 , … , T − t N q_{T-t_{0},\ldots,T-t_{N}} and p t 0 , … , t N p_{t_{0},\ldots,t_{N}} .
Next, we apply the Markovian property of the paths along with Girsanov’s change-of-measure theorem to upper bound 𝖪𝖫 ( q 0 ∥ p T ) \mathsf{KL}(q_{0}\|p_{T}) by
𝖪𝖫 ( q T ∥ p 0 ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 ∫ t k t k + 1 𝔼 x t k , x t ∼ q ← t k , t [ ∑ y t : Q ⁡ ( y t , x t ) > 0 \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(q_{T}\|p_{0})+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{t_{k}},x_{t}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t_{k},t}}\Bigg[\sum_{y_{t}:Q(y_{t},x_{t})>0}
s T − t ​ ( y t , x t ) \displaystyle s_{T-t}(y_{t},x_{t})
× D ( e T − t k − 1 e T − t − 1 s ^ T − t k ( y t , x t ) , s T − t ( y t , x t ) ) d t ] . \displaystyle\times D\left(\frac{e^{T-t_{k}}-1}{e^{T-t}-1}\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}}(y_{t},x_{t}),s_{T-t}(y_{t},x_{t})\right)\mathrm{d}t\Bigg].
The first term is the initialization error and is controlled by choosing the time horizon T = Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ d + log ⁡ log ⁡ ( ε − 1 ​ S ) ) T=\Omega(\log d+\log\log(\varepsilon^{-1}S)) .
For the second term, we apply the law of cosines for Bregman divergence and obtain (with ℓ ≔ t k \ell\coloneqq t_{k} and y ℓ ≔ x ℓ ⊙ i c y_{\ell}\coloneqq x_{\ell}\odot_{i}c , where y t = x t ⊙ i c y_{t}=x_{t}\odot_{i}c ):
s T − t ​ ( y t , x t ) ​ D ​ ( e T − ℓ − 1 e T − t − 1 ​ s ^ T − ℓ ​ ( y t , x t ) , s T − t ​ ( y t , x t ) ) \displaystyle s_{T-t}(y_{t},x_{t})D\left(\frac{e^{T-\ell}-1}{e^{T-t}-1}\widehat{s}_{T-\ell}(y_{t},x_{t}),s_{T-t}(y_{t},x_{t})\right)
= e T − ℓ − 1 e T − t − 1 ​ s T − ℓ ​ ( y ℓ , x ℓ ) ​ D ​ ( s ^ T − ℓ ​ ( y ℓ , x t ) , s T − t ​ ( y t , x t ) ) ⏟ Controlled by Assumption 1 + ( s T − t ​ ( y ℓ , x ℓ ) − s T − t ​ ( y t , x t ) ) ​ log ⁡ s ^ T − ℓ ​ ( y ℓ , x ℓ ) s T − ℓ ​ ( y ℓ , x ℓ ) ⏟ Expectation controlled by Lemma 14 \displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{e^{T-\ell}-1}{e^{T-t}-1}s_{T-\ell}(y_{\ell},x_{\ell})D\left(\widehat{s}_{T-\ell}(y_{\ell},x_{t}),s_{T-t}(y_{t},x_{t})\right)}_{\text{Controlled by~\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{asm:escore}}}+\underbrace{\left(s_{T-t}(y_{\ell},x_{\ell})-s_{T-t}(y_{t},x_{t})\right)\log\frac{\widehat{s}_{T-\ell}(y_{\ell},x_{\ell})}{s_{T-\ell}(y_{\ell},x_{\ell})}}_{\text{Expectation controlled by~\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem:mask_mart}}}
+ s T − t ​ ( y t , x t ) ​ D ​ ( s T − t ​ ( y ℓ , x ℓ ) , s T − t ​ ( y t , x t ) ) . \displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+s_{T-t}(y_{t},x_{t})D\left(s_{T-t}(y_{\ell},x_{\ell}),s_{T-t}(y_{t},x_{t})\right).
Similar to the proof of the uniform discrete diffusion model, the first term can be controlled by Assumption 1 after taking the expectation over x t k ∼ q ← t k x_{t_{k}}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t_{k}} and integrating over time,
and the second term can be proved to be zero by the martingale property from Lemma 14 .
Finally, using Dynkin’s formula, we relate the third term to the effective total correlation
𝒟 ⁡ ( q 0 ) {\mathcal{D}}(q_{0}) .
∎
Next, we derive iteration complexity guarantees for our algorithm under specific choices of step size schedules. The proof is given in Appendix D.2 .
Corollary 2 .
Consider the setting in Theorem 3 . Let T = log ⁡ ( d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) ) T=\log(d\log(S)) . For a fixed ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , the distribution p output p_{\mathrm{output}} satisfies 𝖪𝖫 ( q data ∥ p output ) ≲ ε score + ε \mathsf{KL}(q_{\mathrm{data}}\,\|\,p_{\mathrm{output}})\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{score}}+\varepsilon ,
•
under the constant step size schedule, t k − t k − 1 = T / N t_{k}-t_{k-1}=T/N for all k ∈ [ N ] k\in[N] , provided
N = O ~ ​ ( ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) ε ) ; \displaystyle N=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{{\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}})}{\varepsilon}\right);
•
under the exponential-then-constant step size schedule, when t k + 1 − t k ≤ κ ​ min ⁡ ( 1 , T − t k + 1 ) t_{k+1}-t_{k}\leq\kappa\min(1,T-t_{k+1}) for k ∈ { 0 , … , N − 2 } k\in\{0,\ldots,N-2\} , T − t N − 1 = ε / ( d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) ) T-t_{N-1}=\varepsilon/(d\log(S)) , and κ = N − 1 ​ ( T + log ⁡ ( ε − 1 ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) ) ) \kappa=N^{-1}(T+\log(\varepsilon^{-1}d\log(S))) , provided
N = O ~ ​ ( 𝒟 ⁡ ( q data ) ε ) ≤ O ~ ​ ( min ⁡ { ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) , 𝒞 ⁡ ( q data ) } ε ) . \displaystyle N=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{{\mathcal{D}}(q_{\mathrm{data}})}{\varepsilon}\right)\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\min\{{\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}),{\mathcal{C}}(q_{\mathrm{data}})\}}{\varepsilon}\right).
In words, Corollary 2 shows that the sampling complexity of Algorithm 1 required to obtain an ε \varepsilon -accurate distribution is governed by intrinsic complexity measures of the target distribution.
Under the constant step size schedule, the iteration complexity is controlled by the dual total correlation of the target distribution, whereas under the exponential-then-constant schedule, the effective total correlation becomes the relevant quantity.
For illustration, let us consider the following two simple examples.
•
Consider first the uniform distribution on [ S ] d [S]^{d} . In this case, both complexity measures scale independently of the ambient dimension d d , which means
N = O ~ ​ ( 1 ε ) , \displaystyle N=\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big),
(21)
reflecting the fact that it is exceptionally easy to sample from uniform distributions. While intuitive in hindsight, this phenomenon has not been previously formalized in the literature.
•
As a second example, consider a mixture of two Dirac measures, 1 2 ​ δ k 1 + 1 2 ​ δ k 2 \frac{1}{2}\delta_{k_{1}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k_{2}} . A direct calculation shows that the dual total correlation remains independent of d d , which means
N = O ~ ​ ( 1 ε ) , \displaystyle N=\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big),
(22)
indicating that such distributions are also handled automatically by our algorithm.
To further illustrate the implications of Theorem 3 , we consider some representative distributions for which one or more of the quantities ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) , 𝒞 ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}),{\mathcal{C}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) , or 𝒟 ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{D}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) are small.
Since the iteration complexity scales linearly with these quantities, our result shows that discrete diffusion models can provably achieve efficient sampling.
Appendix A develops these examples in detail and provides rigorous proofs of the stated claims.
•
Hidden Markov models. Here, the observed variables correspond to words or tokens in a sentence, while the hidden states encode latent semantic topics.
Under the natural assumption that topics evolve slowly, we show that ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) grows sublinearly with the sequence length.
•
Low-dimensional structures. Motivated by image generation, when the discrete data arise from the quantization of a continuous distribution with intrinsic dimension k k , the dual total correlation ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) scales linearly with k k rather than with the ambient dimension d d .
•
Random graph models. Such models define distributions over d = ( n 2 ) d={n\choose 2} binary variables corresponding to the edges of a graph with n n vertices. Besides Erdős-Rényi random graphs, which have independent edges and are therefore easy to sample, we consider both sparse random regular graphs and stochastic block models. In these cases, ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) grows at most linearly (up to logarithmic factors) with n n , rather than quadratically.
•
Structure-with-noise distributions. Finally, we present an example in which both the total correlation 𝒞 ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{C}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) and the dual total correlation ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) are of order d d , while the effective total correlation 𝒟 ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{D}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) remains of constant order. Such distributions are motivated by applications such as error-correcting codes and DNA sequences, where substantial noise may be present, yet the underlying signal is highly structured.
4 Discussion
In this work, we establish novel theoretical results for both uniform and masking discrete diffusions. For uniform diffusion models, we show that the τ \tau -leaping algorithm requires O ~ ​ ( d / ε ) \widetilde{O}\left(d/\varepsilon\right) iterations to achieve ε \varepsilon accuracy in KL divergence, improving on the prior bound O ~ ​ ( d 2 ​ S / ε ) \widetilde{O}\left(d^{2}S/\varepsilon\right) . We further establish the first algorithmic lower bound for the τ \tau -leaping sampler, which shows that our upper bound is unimprovable for a large class of distributions.
For the masking discrete diffusion, we derive an upper bound that captures the intrinsic complexity of the data distribution and can scale logarithmically with the ambient dimension. Importantly, our results for both models only require a small score estimation error and, in contrast to prior work, do not rely on early stopping or the boundedness assumptions of the score estimator.
The improved bound for the masking noising process is achieved via a modification of the τ \tau -leaping algorithm. This modification falls within a structured subclass of τ \tau -leaping strategies that (i) allow for parallel coordinate updates, and thus sublinear rates, and (ii) preserve CTMC dynamics, which facilitates theoretical analysis. We hope that this perspective motivates the development of adaptive samplers for uniform discrete diffusion as well in the future.
Several other open questions remain. Understanding which noising mechanisms — masking, uniform, or others
— are best suited to different classes of target distributions is an important direction for future work. Moreover, the problem of learning accurate score functions in discrete diffusion models remains largely unexplored and warrants further investigation.
