Appendix A Examples of low intrinsic dimensions
A.1 Details and formal results
In this section, we revisit the examples outlined in Section 3.2.2 and develop them in full detail. We formalize the statements in this section, and provide rigorous proofs in Appendix A.2 .
Hidden Markov models.
A hidden Markov model (HMM) consists of a latent Markov chain whose states are observed only indirectly through noisy measurements. Such models are widely used in natural language processing and pattern recognition ( Mor et al., 2021 ; Gales and Young, 2024 ) .
In language modeling, for instance, the hidden states z i z^{i} may encode the semantic topic or grammatical structure of the i i -th token or word, while the observed variables x i x^{i} represent the realized words or tokens.
Formally, let { z i } i ∈ [ d ] \{z^{i}\}_{i\in[d]} be a discrete-state Markov chain supported on 𝒵 {\mathcal{Z}} , and let { x i } i ∈ [ d ] \{x^{i}\}_{i\in[d]} be observations generated according to
x i = f i ​ ( z i , ε i ) , x^{i}=f_{i}(z^{i},\varepsilon^{i}),
where { ε i } i ∈ [ d ] \{\varepsilon^{i}\}_{i\in[d]} are i.i.d. noise variables independent of z i i ∈ [ d ] {z^{i}}_{i\in[d]} .
When z i z^{i} represents the semantic topic of the i i -th paragraph in a document, it is natural to assume that topic transitions happen only infrequently; that is, z i = z i − 1 z^{i}=z^{i-1} with high probability for i > 1 i>1 .
Under this model, we establish the following proposition, whose proof is deferred to Section A.2.1 .
Proposition 1 .
Consider the HMM described above. Suppose the transition probability of { z i } i ∈ [ d ] \{z^{i}\}_{i\in[d]} satisfies Pr ⁡ ( z i ≠ z i − 1 ) ≤ p \Pr(z^{i}\neq z^{i-1})\leq p for all i ∈ { 2 , … , d } i\in\{2,\ldots,d\} .
Assume that 1 / d ≲ p ≪ 1 1/d\lesssim p\ll 1 . Then
ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) ≤ p ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( | 𝒵 | p ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{B}(q_{\mathrm{data}})\leq pd\log\left(\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|}{p}\right).
(23)
To develop some intuition, consider generating a document with a constant number of paragraphs, where the transition probability scales p = Θ ⁡ ( 1 / d ) p=\Theta(1/d) . Suppose further that the latent space 𝒵 ∈ [ S ] k \mathcal{Z}\in[S]^{k} for some k ≪ d k\ll d and S S denotes the vocabulary size. Then, the above bound yields
ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) ≲ k ​ log ⁡ ( S ​ d ) , \mathcal{B}(q_{\mathrm{data}})\lesssim k\log(Sd),
which is substantially smaller than the ambient dimension d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) d\log(S) . As such, with Theorem 3 , the sampling complexity scales with the intrinsic topic dimension k k rather than the document length d d .
Low-dimensional Structures.
In image generation and other structured data settings, it is commonly assumed that the data lie on or near a low-dimensional manifold embedded in a high-dimensional ambient space, which often refers to as the manifold hypothesis ( Gorban and Tyukin, 2018 ; Pope et al., 2021 ) .
For example, natural images may be viewed as points on a manifold parameterized by a small number of underlying factors, such as lighting conditions, pose, and object identity.
In discrete settings, the notion of a manifold is not mathematically well defined. To capture low-dimensional structure, we instead model the data as arising from a continuous mapping from a latent representation into a high-dimensional observation space.
For some latent continuous random variable z z supported on 𝒵 ⊂ ℝ k {\mathcal{Z}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k} , consider a decoding procedure f : [ 0 , 1 ] k → ℝ d f:[0,1]^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d} as
x con = f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise , x^{\mathrm{con}}=f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}},
for additive perturbations ε noise . \varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}.
Thus, data lies close to a manifold { f ⁡ ( z ) : z ∈ 𝒵 } \{f(z):z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\} .
The final discrete observation is obtained via a quantization operator 𝒬 S \mathcal{Q}_{S} , i.e., x = 𝒬 S ​ ( x con ) ∼ q data x=\mathcal{Q}_{S}(x^{\mathrm{con}})\sim q_{\mathrm{data}} .
To align the model with standard image processing pipelines, we work with the uniform lattice quantization function 𝒬 S : ℝ d → [ S ] d \mathcal{Q}_{S}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[S]^{d} defined coordinate-wise as [ 𝒬 S ​ ( x ) ] i = clip ⁡ ( ⌊ x i ⌋ , 0 , S ) [\mathcal{Q}_{S}(x)]^{i}=\mathrm{clip}(\lfloor x^{i}\rfloor,0,S) for i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , where clip ⁡ ( x , a , b ) ≔ min ⁡ { max ⁡ { x , a } , b } \mathrm{clip}(x,a,b)\coloneqq\min\{\max\{x,a\},b\} is the clip function and ⌊ ⋅ ⌋ \lfloor\cdot\rfloor is the floor function.
To ensure regularity of both the manifold and the induced data distribution, we focus on the case where 𝒵 {\mathcal{Z}} is a compact set and f f is a Lipschitz function.
The noise ε noise \varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}} is taken to be Gaussian for simplicity of analysis; the arguments extend readily to more general smooth noise distributions.
Proposition 2 .
Let 𝒵 ⊂ ℝ k {\mathcal{Z}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k} be compact with diameter 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , and let
f : 𝒵 → ℝ d f:{\mathcal{Z}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d} be 𝖫 \mathsf{L} -Lipschitz.
Assume the noise satisfies ε noise ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ​ I d ) \varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d}) independenly generated for each obersvation.
Then the resulting distribution satisfies
ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) ≤ k ​ log ⁡ ( 2 + 𝟤 ​ 𝖣 ​ 𝖫 σ ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{B}(q_{\mathrm{data}})\leq k\log\left(2+\frac{\mathsf{2D}\mathsf{L}}{\sigma}\right).
(24)
In image generation, the “ideal image” x con x^{\mathrm{con}} may be interpreted as the vector of continuous pixel intensities prior to quantization, while the observed image x x is obtained by applying pixel-wise quantization to x con x^{\mathrm{con}} .
When k ≪ d k\ll d , the above bound yields
ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) = O ~ ​ ( k ) = o ⁡ ( d ) , \mathcal{B}(q_{\mathrm{data}})=\widetilde{O}(k)=o(d),
and hence we can efficiently sample such images despite the high dimensionality of the observation space.
Random graph models.
Discrete diffusion models have also found applications in scientific domains such as molecular generation and protein design, where data are naturally represented as random graphs with fixed vertex sets and random edges ( Ingraham et al., 2019 ; Xu et al., 2022 ) .
To make this concrete, we consider two widely studied random graph models on n n vertices, which can be viewed as a discrete distribution over adjacency matrices of dimension n 2 . n^{2}.
•
Regular graphs : A k k -regular graph is a graph in which each vertex has degree exactly k k .
Suppose we want to sample a random graph 𝒢 \mathcal{G} from some distribution supported on the set of k k -regular graphs with n n vertices.
Proposition 3 .
For sparse regular graph model, i.e., k ≤ n / log ⁡ ( n ) k\leq n/\log(n) , we have
ℬ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ≲ k ​ n ​ log ⁡ ( n k ) = o ⁡ ( n 2 ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})\lesssim kn\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)=o(n^{2}).
(25)
•
Stochastic block models : A stochastic block model (SBM) is a generative model for random graphs that captures community structure within networks.
In an SBM, n n vertices of the graph are partitioned into r r distinct communities or blocks, represented by latent variables { z i } i ∈ [ n ] \{z^{i}\}_{i\in[n]} taking values in [ r ] [r] .
Conditioned on the latent labels, edges are generated independently. For two vertices i , j ∈ [ n ] i,j\in[n] , an edge is created with probability
p 𝕀 { z i = z j } + q 𝕀 { z i ≠ z j } , p{\mathbb{I}}\{z^{i}=z^{j}\}+q{\mathbb{I}}\{z^{i}\neq z^{j}\},
where p , q ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] p,q\in[0,1] govern the within- and between-community connection probabilities, respectively.
Proposition 4 .
Let 𝒢 {\mathcal{G}} be a random graph drawn from the above r r -block SBM.
Then
ℬ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ≤ n ​ log ⁡ ( r ) = o ⁡ ( n 2 ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})\leq n\log(r)=o(n^{2}).
For both random graph models, as the number of vertices n n grows large, the complexity satisfies ℬ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) = o ⁡ ( n 2 ) \mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})=o(n^{2}) , which is strictly smaller compared to the ambient dimension n 2 . n^{2}.
This indicates that diffusion-based methods can sample efficiently from such graph distributions.
In fact, the analyses of Propositions 2 and 4 extend naturally to generalized random geometric graphs. Consider the following example.
Let each vertex i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] be associated with latent variable z i ∈ 𝒵 z^{i}\in{\mathcal{Z}} . For distinct vertices i i and j j , an edge is placed independently with probability
β ​ exp ⁡ ( − d ⁡ ( z i , z j ) r 0 ) , \displaystyle\beta\exp\left(-\frac{d(z^{i},z^{j})}{r_{0}}\right),
where β ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] , \beta\in[0,1], r 0 > 0 r_{0}>0 and d ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) d(\cdot,\cdot) is an appropriate metric in the latent space 𝒵 {\mathcal{Z}} .
•
When latent variables { z i } \{z^{i}\} are discrete with o ⁡ ( n ) o(n) entropy, as is the case, for example, when it takes value in a fixed-dimensional latent space, the dual total correlation of the resulting random graph is o ⁡ ( n 2 ) o(n^{2}) .
•
For continuous latent variables, suppose 𝒵 = 𝒮 d z − 1 {\mathcal{Z}}={{\mathcal{S}}}^{d_{z}-1} , the unit sphere in ℝ d z \mathbb{R}^{d_{z}} .
Under some regularity conditions,
the dual total correlation scales with d z ⋅ n d_{z}\cdot n , followed by an analogous covering number argument in Proposition 2 . In particular, whenever d z = o ⁡ ( n ) d_{z}=o(n) , the complexity is again subquadratic, leading to sublinear (in n 2 n^{2} ) convergence rates for diffusion-based sampling.
Structure-with-noise distributions
A prototypical example of a distribution with small dual total correlation ℬ ⁡ ( q 0 ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{0}) and large total correlation 𝒞 ⁡ ( q 0 ) {\mathcal{C}}(q_{0}) is the mixture of two Dirac measures:
p m ≔ 1 2 ​ δ 𝟎 + 1 2 ​ δ 𝟏 , p_{m}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathbf{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathbf{1}},
where 𝟎 \mathbf{0} and 𝟏 \mathbf{1} are vectors of all-zeros and all-ones, respectively. It can be easily computed that ℬ ⁡ ( p m ) = log ⁡ ( 2 ) {\mathcal{B}}(p_{m})=\log(2) , whereas, 𝒞 ⁡ ( p m ) = ( d − 1 ) ​ log ⁡ ( 2 ) {\mathcal{C}}(p_{m})=(d-1)\log(2) .
The opposite happens, for instance, for the following XOR \mathrm{XOR} distribution p XOR p_{\mathrm{XOR}} :
x 1 , … , x d − 1 ​ ∼ i . i . d . ​ Bern ​ ( 1 / 2 ) ​ and ​ x d = ∑ i = 1 d − 1 x i mod 2 . x^{1},\ldots,x^{d-1}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathrm{Bern}(1/2)~~\text{ and }~~x^{d}=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}x^{i}\bmod 2.
In this case, ℬ ⁡ ( p XOR ) = ( d − 1 ) ​ log ⁡ ( 2 ) {\mathcal{B}}(p_{\mathrm{XOR}})=(d-1)\log(2) , and 𝒞 ⁡ ( p XOR ) = log ⁡ ( 2 ) {\mathcal{C}}(p_{\mathrm{XOR}})=\log(2) .
Real-world data distributions can combine features of both extremes: a strong low-dimensional signal corrupted by weakly correlated noise. In such cases, both ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) and 𝒞 ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{C}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) can be large, while 𝒟 ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{D}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) remains small.
To illustrate this phenomenon, consider the following entrywise mixture of the two preceding examples.
1.
Fix a bi-partition [ d ] = I 0 ⊔ I 1 [d]=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1} for non-empty index sets I 0 I_{0} and I 1 I_{1} ;
2.
For all indices i ∈ I 0 i\in I_{0} , set x i = b x_{i}=b for b ∼ Bern ⁡ ( 1 / 2 ) b\sim\mathrm{Bern}(1/2) ;
3.
Among all indices i ∈ I 1 i\in I_{1} , sample all but one x i ∼ Bern ⁡ ( 1 / 2 ) x_{i}\sim\mathrm{Bern}(1/2) independently;
4.
For the last index i ∗ i^{\ast} , set x i ∗ = ( b + ∑ i ≠ i ∗ x i ) mod 2 x_{i^{\ast}}=\left(b+\sum_{i\neq i^{\ast}}x_{i}\right)\bmod 2 .
Denote this distribution as p ex p_{\mathrm{ex}} , and let x = ( x 1 , … , x d ) ∼ p ex x=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{d})\sim p_{\mathrm{ex}} .
Proposition 5 .
Suppose that min ⁡ { | I 0 | , | I 1 | } / d = Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) \min\{|I_{0}|,|I_{1}|\}/d=\Theta(1) .
Distribution p ex p_{\mathrm{ex}} satisfies
ℬ ( p ex ) = Θ ( d ) , 𝒞 ( p ex ) = Θ ( d ) and 𝒟 ( p ex ) = O ( 1 ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=\Theta(d),\quad{\mathcal{C}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=\Theta(d)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{D}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=O(1).
(26)
By Proposition 5 , p ex p_{\mathrm{ex}} , which can be viewed as a non-trivial mixing of p m p_{m} and p XOR p_{\mathrm{XOR}} , satisfies
𝒟 ⁡ ( p ex ) ≪ min ⁡ { ℬ ⁡ ( p ex ) , 𝒞 ⁡ ( p ex ) } . {\mathcal{D}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})\ll\min\{{\mathcal{B}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}}),{\mathcal{C}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})\}.
This example highlights the fundamental role of the effective total correlation in characterizing sampling efficiency.
A.2 Proofs of results in Section A.1
Variants of the following lemma will be used repeatedly throughout this section. We state it here for convenience and to streamline the proofs.
Lemma 1 .
Consider any d d -dimensional discrete random variable X X and any random variable W W
such that X i ⟂ ⟂ X − i | W X^{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp X^{-i}\mid W for any i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , where X = ( X 1 , … , X d ) X=(X^{1},\ldots,X^{d}) and X − i X^{-i} is the ( d − 1 ) (d-1) -dimensional marginal of X X with i i -th coordinate excluded. Then,
ℬ ⁡ ( X ) ≤ I ⁡ ( X , W ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(X)\leq\mathrm{I}(X;W).
If W W is discrete, we additionally have ℬ ⁡ ( X ) ≤ ℋ ⁡ ( W ) {\mathcal{B}}(X)\leq{\mathcal{H}}(W) .
Proof of Lemma 1 .
We first notice that for any random variable W W such that X i ⟂ ⟂ X − i | W X^{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp X^{-i}\mid W for any i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , we have
ℋ ⁡ ( X i ∣ X − i ) ≥ ℋ ⁡ ( X i ∣ X − i , W ) = ℋ ⁡ ( X i ∣ W ) , \displaystyle\mathcal{H}(X^{i}\mid X^{-i})\geq\mathcal{H}(X^{i}\mid X^{-i},W)=\mathcal{H}(X^{i}\mid W),
where the first inequality is from the definition of the entropy.
Recalling the definition of ℬ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) {\mathcal{B}}(\cdot) , we obtain
ℬ ⁡ ( X ) = ℋ ⁡ ( X ) − ∑ i = 1 d ℋ ⁡ ( X i | X − i ) ≤ ℋ ⁡ ( X ) − ∑ i = 1 d ℋ ⁡ ( X i ∣ W ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{B}(X)={\mathcal{H}}(X)-\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}(X^{i}|X^{-i})\leq\mathcal{H}(X)-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{H}(X^{i}\mid W).
Using the conditional independence condition again, we have
ℋ ⁡ ( X | W ) = ℋ ⁡ ( ( X 1 , … , X d ) | W ) = ∑ i = 1 d ℋ ⁡ ( X i ∣ W ) , \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(X|W)={\mathcal{H}}\big((X_{1},\ldots,X_{d})|W\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{H}(X^{i}\mid W),
which implies
ℬ ⁡ ( X ) ≤ ℋ ⁡ ( X ) − ℋ ⁡ ( X | W ) = I ⁡ ( X , W ) ​ = ( a ) ​ ℋ ​ ( W ) − ℋ ⁡ ( W | X ) ​ ≤ ( b ) ​ ℋ ​ ( W ) , \displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(X)\leq{\mathcal{H}}(X)-{\mathcal{H}}(X|W)=I(X;W)\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{=}{\mathcal{H}}(W)-{\mathcal{H}}(W|X)\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{\leq}{\mathcal{H}}(W),
where (a) and (b) apply when W W is a discrete random variable.
∎
A.2.1 Proof of Proposition 1
For the hidden Markov structure of { ( x i , z i ) } i ∈ [ d ] \{(x^{i},z^{i})\}_{i\in[d]} , it satisfies x i ⟂ ⟂ x j | ( z i , z j ) x^{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp x^{j}\mid(z^{i},z^{j}) , since ε i ⟂ ⟂ ε j | ( z i , z j ) \varepsilon^{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\varepsilon^{j}\mid(z^{i},z^{j}) .
Considering Lemma 1 above, we can upper bound ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) {\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}}) by ℋ ⁡ ( z ) {\mathcal{H}}(z) , which is the entropy of the latent Markov chain. By the additivity of the entropy, we have
ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) ≤ ℋ ⁡ ( z ) = ℋ ⁡ ( z 1 ) + ∑ i = 2 d ℋ ⁡ ( z i | { z j } j ∈ [ i − 1 ] ) = ℋ ⁡ ( z 1 ) + ∑ i = 2 d ℋ ⁡ ( z i | z i − 1 ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}})\leq{\mathcal{H}}(z)={\mathcal{H}}(z^{1})+\sum_{i=2}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}\big(z^{i}|\{z^{j}\}_{j\in[i-1]}\big)={\mathcal{H}}(z^{1})+\sum_{i=2}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}(z^{i}|z^{i-1}).
When { z i } i ∈ [ d ] \{z^{i}\}_{i\in[d]} is supported on a single point, we have | 𝒵 | = 1 |{\mathcal{Z}}|=1 and ℋ ⁡ ( z ) = 0 {\mathcal{H}}(z)=0 .
When the state space 𝒵 {\mathcal{Z}} satisfies 2 ≤ | 𝒵 | < ∞ 2\leq|{\mathcal{Z}}|<\infty , the maximum entropy distribution is achieved when
z 1 ∼ Unif ⁡ ( 𝒵 ) and z i | z i − 1 ∼ ( 1 − p ) ​ δ z i − 1 + p ​ Unif ​ ( 𝒵 \ { z i − 1 } ) . \displaystyle z^{1}\sim\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{Z}})\quad\text{and}\quad z^{i}|z^{i-1}\sim(1-p)\delta_{z^{i-1}}+p\mathrm{Unif}\big({\mathcal{Z}}\,\backslash\,\{z^{i-1}\}\big).
We obtain
ℋ ⁡ ( z ) \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(z)
≤ log ⁡ ( | 𝒵 | ) + ∑ i = 2 d [ − ( 1 − p ) ​ log ⁡ ( 1 − p ) + − ( | 𝒵 | − 1 ) ⋅ p | 𝒵 | − 1 ​ log ⁡ ( p | 𝒵 | − 1 ) ] \displaystyle\leq\log(|{\mathcal{Z}}|)+\sum_{i=2}^{d}\left[-(1-p)\log(1-p)+-(|{\mathcal{Z}}|-1)\cdot\frac{p}{|{\mathcal{Z}}|-1}\log\left(\frac{p}{|{\mathcal{Z}}|-1}\right)\right]
≤ ( a ) ​ log ⁡ ( | 𝒵 | ) + ( d − 1 ) ⋅ ( 2 ​ p + p ​ log ⁡ ( | 𝒵 | p ) ) \displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\leq}\log(|{\mathcal{Z}}|)+(d-1)\cdot\left(2p+p\log\left(\frac{|{\mathcal{Z}}|}{p}\right)\right)
≤ ( b ) ​ p ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( | 𝒵 | p ) , \displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{\leq}pd\log\left(\frac{|{\mathcal{Z}}|}{p}\right),
where in (a), we use − log ⁡ ( 1 − p ) ≤ 2 ​ p -\log(1-p)\leq 2p , since p ≪ 1 p\ll 1 ; in (b), we use the condition p ≳ 1 / d p\gtrsim 1/d and | 𝒵 | / p ≥ 2 / p ≫ 1 |{\mathcal{Z}}|/p\geq 2/p\gg 1 . This completes the proof of the desired result.
∎
A.2.2 Proof of Proposition 2
Write ε noise = ( ε noise 1 , … , ε noise d ) \varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}=(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}^{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}^{d}) . Since ε noise ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ​ I d ) \varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d}) , we have ε noise i ⟂ ⟂ ε noise − i \varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}^{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}^{-i} for any i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] . Processing through the decoder f f , [ x con ] i = [ f ⁡ ( z ) ] i + ε noise i [x^{\mathrm{con}}]^{i}=[f(z)]^{i}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}^{i} for any i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , which leads to
[ x con ] i ⟂ ⟂ [ x con ] ( − i ) | z , \displaystyle[x^{\mathrm{con}}]^{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp[x^{\mathrm{con}}]^{(-i)}\mid z,
(27)
where [ x con ] ( − i ) [x^{\mathrm{con}}]^{(-i)} is the ( d − 1 ) (d-1) -dimensional marginal of x con x^{\mathrm{con}} with i i -th coordinate excluded.
Note that 𝒬 S {\mathcal{Q}}_{S} is a entry-wise quantization, i.e., we can write 𝒬 S ​ ( x ) = ( 𝒬 ~ S ​ ( x 1 ) , … , 𝒬 ~ S ​ ( x d ) ) {\mathcal{Q}}_{S}(x)=(\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{S}(x^{1}),\ldots,\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{S}(x^{d})) for entry-wise deterministic quantization function 𝒬 ~ S : ℝ → [ S ] \widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{S}:\mathbb{R}\to[S] , and x i = 𝒬 ~ S ​ ( [ x con ] i ) x^{i}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{S}([x^{\mathrm{con}}]^{i}) by the generation process.
Eqn. ( 27 ) therefore implies that for any i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] ,
x i ⟂ ⟂ x − i | z . \displaystyle x^{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp x^{-i}\mid z.
Applying Lemma 1 , we obtain
ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) = ℬ ⁡ ( x ) ≤ I ⁡ ( x , z ) ≤ I ⁡ ( x con , z ) , \displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}})={\mathcal{B}}(x)\leq\mathrm{I}(x;z)\leq\mathrm{I}(x^{\mathrm{con}};z),
(28)
where the last inequality follows from the data processing inequality of the mutual information.
In the following proof, we proceed to control I ⁡ ( x con , z ) \mathrm{I}(x^{\mathrm{con}};z) . Since ε noise \varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}} is independent noise, using data-processing inequality, we reach
I ⁡ ( x con , z ) ≤ I ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise , f ⁡ ( z ) ) = I ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( z ) , f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ) . \displaystyle\mathrm{I}(x^{\mathrm{con}};z)\leq\mathrm{I}\big(f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}};f(z)\big)=\mathrm{I}\big(f(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\big).
(29)
Without loss of generality, we assume 𝒵 ⊆ [ 0 , 𝖣 ] k {\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq[0,\mathsf{D}]^{k} .
Partition [ 0 , 𝖣 ] k [0,\mathsf{D}]^{k} into hypercubes of size h J = σ / 𝖫 h_{J}=\sigma/\mathsf{L} , and write this partition as { C 1 , … , C ⌈ 𝖣 / h J ⌉ k } \{C_{1},\ldots,C_{\lceil\mathsf{D}/h_{J}\rceil^{k}}\} such that
[ 0 , 𝖣 ] k ⊆ ⨆ i = 1 ⌈ 𝖣 / h J ⌉ k C i . [0,\mathsf{D}]^{k}\subseteq\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\lceil{\mathsf{D}}/{h_{J}}\rceil^{k}}C_{i}.
Define J = J ⁡ ( z ) J=J(z) to be the hypercube index i ⁡ ( z ) i(z) such that z ∈ C i ⁡ ( z ) z\in C_{i(z)} , and ℱ J {\mathcal{F}}_{J} to be σ \sigma -algebra generated by J ⁡ ( z ) J(z) .
By the chain rule and data processing inequality for mutual information, we have
I ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( z ) , f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ) \displaystyle\mathrm{I}\big(f(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\big)
≤ I ⁡ ( J ⁡ ( z ) , f ⁡ ( z ) , f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ) \displaystyle\leq\mathrm{I}\big(J(z),f(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\big)
= I ⁡ ( J ⁡ ( z ) , f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ) + I ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( z ) ; f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ∣ J ) \displaystyle=\mathrm{I}\big(J(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\big)+\mathrm{I}\big(f(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\mid J\big)
≤ k ​ log ⁡ ( 1 + 𝖣 h J ) + I ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( z ) ; f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ∣ J ) , \displaystyle\leq k\log\left(1+\frac{\mathsf{D}}{h_{J}}\right)+\mathrm{I}\big(f(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\mid J\big),
(30)
where in the last line, we use I ⁡ ( J ⁡ ( z ) , f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ) ≤ ℋ ⁡ ( J ⁡ ( z ) ) ≤ log ⁡ ( | supp ⁡ ( J ⁡ ( z ) ) | ) \mathrm{I}(J(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}})\leq{\mathcal{H}}(J(z))\leq\log(|\mathrm{supp}(J(z))|) . To upper bound the second term above, we introduce the following lemma on Gaussian channel, whose proof is given in Section F.1 .
Lemma 2 .
For any random variable W ∈ ℝ d W\in\mathbb{R}^{d} and independent noise ε noise ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ​ I d ) \varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d}) , we have
I ⁡ ( W , W + ε noise ) ≤ Tr ⁡ ( Var ⁡ [ W ] ) 2 ​ σ 2 , \displaystyle\mathrm{I}(W;W+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}})\leq\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\big(\mathrm{Var}[W]\big)}{2\sigma^{2}},
where Tr ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathrm{Tr}(\cdot) is the trace function.
In Lemma 2 , taking W ​ = 𝑑 ​ f ​ ( z ) | ℱ J W\overset{d}{=}f(z)\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{J} , we arrive at
I ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( z ) ; f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ∣ J ) ≤ Tr ⁡ ( Var ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( z ) ∣ ℱ J ] ) 2 ​ σ 2 . \displaystyle\mathrm{I}\big(f(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\mid J\big)\leq\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\Big(\mathrm{Var}\big[f(z)\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{J}\big]\Big)}{2\sigma^{2}}.
(31)
To further control the right hand side,
direct calculations show
Tr ⁡ ( Var ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( z ) ∣ ℱ J ] ) \displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\Big(\mathrm{Var}\big[f(z)\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{J}\big]\Big)
= ∑ i = 1 d Var ⁡ [ [ f ⁡ ( z ) ] i | ℱ J ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ ‖ f ⁡ ( z ) − 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( z ) ∣ ℱ J ] ‖ 2 2 | ℱ J ] . \displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathrm{Var}\big[[f(z)]^{i}\,\big|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{J}\big]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big\|f(z)-\mathbb{E}\big[f(z)\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{J}\big]\Big\|_{2}^{2}\,\Big|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{J}\bigg].
(32)
It is therefore sufficient to consider the quantity ‖ f ⁡ ( z ) − 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( z ) ∣ ℱ J ] ‖ 2 2 . \|f(z)-\mathbb{E}[f(z)\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{J}]\|_{2}^{2}. We make the observation that
‖ f ⁡ ( z ) − 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( z ) ∣ ℱ J ] ‖ 2 \displaystyle\Big\|f(z)-\mathbb{E}\big[f(z)\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{J}\big]\Big\|_{2}
≤ ( a ) ​ sup w ∈ Conv ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( C J ⁡ ( z ) ) ) ‖ f ⁡ ( z ) − w ‖ 2 \displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\leq}\sup_{w\in\mathrm{Conv}(f(C_{J(z)}))}\big\|f(z)-w\big\|_{2}
= ( b ) ​ sup w ∈ f ⁡ ( C J ⁡ ( z ) ) ‖ f ⁡ ( z ) − w ‖ 2 \displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{=}\sup_{w\in f(C_{J(z)})}\big\|f(z)-w\big\|_{2}
≤ ( c ) ​ ‖ f ‖ Lip ⋅ sup z ′ ∈ C J ⁡ ( z ) ‖ z − z ′ ‖ 2 ​ ≤ ( d ) ​ 𝖫 ​ k ​ h J , \displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(c)}}{\leq}\|f\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}\cdot\sup_{z^{\prime}\in C_{J(z)}}\big\|z-z^{\prime}\big\|_{2}\overset{\mathrm{(d)}}{\leq}\mathsf{L}\sqrt{k}h_{J},
where ∥ ⋅ ∥ 2 \|\cdot\|_{2} denotes Euclidean norm in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , and Conv ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathrm{Conv}(\cdot) denotes the convex null of a given set.
In (a), we use the fact that 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( z ) ∣ ℱ J ] ∈ Conv ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( C J ⁡ ( z ) ) ) \mathbb{E}[f(z)\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{J}]\in\mathrm{Conv}(f(C_{J(z)})) ; in (b), we adopt f f is continuous and hence OPEN f ⁡ ( C J ⁡ ( z ) ) ) f(C_{J(z)})) is bounded, and the property of the convex hull that
diam ⁡ ( Conv ⁡ ( A ) ) = diam ⁡ ( A ) for any bounded subset ​ A ⊆ ℝ d ; \displaystyle\mathrm{diam}\big(\mathrm{Conv}(A)\big)=\mathrm{diam}(A)\quad\text{for any bounded subset }A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d};
in (c), we recall the Lipschitz condition on f f ; in (d), we notice that diam ⁡ ( C i ) ≤ k ​ h J \mathrm{diam}(C_{i})\leq\sqrt{k}h_{J} for any hypercube C i C_{i} .
Putting pieces together gives
Tr ⁡ ( Var ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( z ) ∣ ℱ J ] ) \displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\Big(\mathrm{Var}\big[f(z)\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{J}\big]\Big)
≤ ( 𝖫 ​ k ​ h J ) 2 = k ​ σ 2 . \displaystyle\leq\big(\mathsf{L}\sqrt{k}h_{J}\big)^{2}=k\sigma^{2}.
(33)
Finally, plugging Eqns. ( 31 ) and ( 33 ) into Equation 30 , we obtain
I ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( z ) , f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ) ≤ k ​ log ⁡ ( 1 + 𝖣𝖫 σ ) + k 2 ≤ k ​ log ⁡ ( 2 + 𝟤 ​ 𝖣 ​ 𝖫 σ ) . \displaystyle\mathrm{I}\big(f(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\big)\leq k\log\left(1+\frac{\mathsf{D}\mathsf{L}}{\sigma}\right)+\frac{k}{2}\leq k\log\left(2+\frac{\mathsf{2D}\mathsf{L}}{\sigma}\right).
Combining the above inequality with Eqns. ( 28 ) and ( 29 ), we conclude
ℬ ⁡ ( q data ) ≤ I ⁡ ( x con , z ) = I ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( z ) , f ⁡ ( z ) + ε noise ) ≤ k ​ log ⁡ ( 2 + 𝟤 ​ 𝖣 ​ 𝖫 σ ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(q_{\mathrm{data}})\leq\mathrm{I}(x^{\mathrm{con}};z)=\mathrm{I}\big(f(z);f(z)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{noise}}\big)\leq k\log\left(2+\frac{\mathsf{2D}\mathsf{L}}{\sigma}\right).
∎
A.2.3 Proof of Proposition 3
Define the set of all k k -regular graphs with n n vertices as G n , k G_{n,k} . Without loss of generality, we assume that n ​ k nk is even, as otherwise G n , k G_{n,k} is empty.
By a corollary of Liebenau and Wormald (2024, Theorem 1.4) , we have the following asymptotic result:
| G n , k | = Θ ⁡ ( ( n − 1 k ) n ​ ( n ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 2 m ) ​ ( n ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 2 ​ m ) − 1 ) . \displaystyle|G_{n,k}|=\Theta\left({n-1\choose k}^{n}{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\choose m}{n(n-1)\choose 2m}^{-1}\right).
where m = k ​ n / 2 m=kn/2 . By Stirling’s formula of the form
log ⁡ ( a ! ) = a ​ log ⁡ ( a ) − a + O ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( a ) ) , \displaystyle\log(a!)=a\log(a)-a+O(\log(a)),
we can compute that
log ⁡ ( | G n , k | ) \displaystyle\log(|G_{n,k}|)
≲ n ​ log ⁡ ( n − 1 k ) + log ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 2 m ) − log ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 2 ​ m ) \displaystyle\lesssim n\log{n-1\choose k}+\log{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\choose m}-\log{n(n-1)\choose 2m}
= k ​ n 2 ​ log ⁡ ( n − 1 − k k ) + n ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 2 ​ log ⁡ ( n − 1 n − 1 − k ) \displaystyle=\frac{kn}{2}\log\left(\frac{n-1-k}{k}\right)+\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\log\left(\frac{n-1}{n-1-k}\right)
≤ k ​ n 2 ​ log ⁡ ( n k ) + n 2 2 ​ log ⁡ ( 1 + k n − 1 − k ) \displaystyle\leq\frac{kn}{2}\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)+\frac{n^{2}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{k}{n-1-k}\right)
≤ k ​ n 2 ​ log ⁡ ( n k ) + k ​ n 2 2 ​ ( n − 1 − k ) ≲ k ​ n ​ log ⁡ ( n k ) , \displaystyle\leq\frac{kn}{2}\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)+\frac{kn^{2}}{2(n-1-k)}\lesssim kn\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right),
where in the last line, we invoke the condition that k ≤ n / log ⁡ ( n ) ≪ n − 1 − k k\leq n/\log(n)\ll n-1-k .
Recalling the definition of ℬ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) {\mathcal{B}}(\cdot) , we can conclude
ℬ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ≤ ℋ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ≤ log ⁡ ( | G n , k | ) ≲ k ​ n ​ log ⁡ ( n k ) = o ⁡ ( n 2 ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})\leq\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{G})\leq\log(|G_{n,k}|)\lesssim kn\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)=o(n^{2}).
∎
A.2.4 Proof of Proposition 4
By definition of r r -block SBM, the latent variable vector ( z 1 , … , z n ) (z^{1},\ldots,z^{n}) is supported on [ r ] n [r]^{n} , which satisfies
ℋ ⁡ ( ( , , , , , ) ) ≤ log ⁡ ( | [ r ] n | ) = n ​ log ⁡ ( r ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}\big((z^{1},\ldots,z^{n})\big)\leq\log\big(|[r]^{n}|\big)=n\log(r).
Given the latent variable ( z 1 , … , z n ) (z^{1},\ldots,z^{n}) , the block structure is fixed and hence each edge is sampled independently from a Bernoulli distribution. Therefore, we have
e i ​ j ⟂ ⟂ e k ​ ℓ | { z i } i ∈ [ n ] \displaystyle e^{ij}\perp\!\!\!\perp e^{k\ell}\mid\{z^{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
for any i , j , k , l ∈ [ n ] i,j,k,l\in[n] , where e i ​ j e^{ij} and e k ​ ℓ e^{k\ell} are the indicator variables of the existence of edges between vertices i , j i,j and between vertices k , ℓ k,\ell .
By Lemma 1 , we conclude
ℬ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ≤ ℋ ⁡ ( ( , , , , , ) ) ≤ n ​ log ⁡ ( r ) ≤ n ​ log ⁡ ( n ) = o ⁡ ( n 2 ) , \displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})\leq\mathcal{H}((z^{1},\ldots,z^{n}))\leq n\log(r)\leq n\log(n)=o(n^{2}),
where we use the convention that the number of blocks satisfies r ≤ n r\leq n .
∎
Remark 2 .
The setting of Proposition 4 can be viewed as a special case of the generalized random geometric graph model, in which the latent variable corresponds to the block index.
More generally, the same conclusion holds under analogous assumptions, with essentially the same proof strategy.
A.2.5 Proof of Proposition 5
Let r := | I 0 | / d r:=|I_{0}|/d be the proportion of coordinates in I 0 I_{0} . Throughout, we assume min ⁡ { r , 1 − r } = Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) \min\{r,1-r\}=\Theta(1) .
Step 1: Establish ℬ ⁡ ( p ex ) = Θ ⁡ ( d ) {\mathcal{B}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=\Theta(d) and 𝒞 ⁡ ( p ex ) = Θ ⁡ ( d ) {\mathcal{C}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=\Theta(d) .
For a random variable x ∼ p ex x\sim p_{\mathrm{ex}} , we shall demonstrate that
∑ i = 1 d ℋ ( x i ) = d log ( 2 ) , log ( 2 ) ( | I 1 | − 1 ) ≤ ℋ ( x ) ≤ log ( 2 ) | I 1 | and ∑ i = 1 d ℋ ( x i ∣ x − i ) = 0 . \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}(x^{i})=d\log(2),\quad\log(2)(|I_{1}|-1)\leq{\mathcal{H}}(x)\leq\log(2)|I_{1}|\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}(x^{i}\mid x^{-i})=0.
(34)
Towards this goal, we make the observation that for any i ∈ I 0 i\in I_{0} or x ∈ I 1 \ i ∗ x\in I_{1}\backslash i^{*} , x i ∼ Bern ⁡ ( 1 / 2 ) x^{i}\sim\mathrm{Bern}(1/2) and hence ℋ ⁡ ( x i ) = log ⁡ ( 2 ) {\mathcal{H}}(x^{i})=\log(2) . For i = i ∗ i=i^{*} , we assert that x i ∗ ∼ Bern ⁡ ( 1 / 2 ) x^{i^{*}}\sim\mathrm{Bern}(1/2) . In fact, we have
ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ i ∈ I 1 \ i ∗ x i ≡ 0 mod 2 ) = ℙ ⁡ ( Bin ⁡ ( | I 1 | − 1 , 1 2 ) ≡ 0 mod 2 ) = 1 2 , \displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{1}\backslash i^{*}}x^{i}\equiv 0\bmod 2\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Bin}\left(|I_{1}|-1,\frac{1}{2}\right)\equiv 0\bmod 2\right)=\frac{1}{2},
where in the last equality, we invoke the following lemma.
Lemma 3 .
For any n ∈ ℕ + n\in\mathbb{N}^{+} and X ∼ Bin ⁡ ( n , 1 / 2 ) X\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,1/2) , we have
ℙ ⁡ ( X ≡ 0 mod 2 ) = ℙ ⁡ ( X ≡ 1 mod 2 ) = 1 2 . \displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X\equiv 0\bmod 2)=\mathbb{P}(X\equiv 1\bmod 2)=\frac{1}{2}.
As result, the distribution of x i ∗ x^{i^{*}} satisfies
ℙ ⁡ ( x i ∗ = 0 ) = ℙ ⁡ ( b = 0 ) ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ i ∈ I 1 \ i ∗ x i ≡ 0 mod 2 ) + ℙ ⁡ ( b = 1 ) ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ i ∈ I 1 \ i ∗ x i ≡ 1 mod 2 ) = 1 2 , \displaystyle\mathbb{P}(x^{i^{*}}=0)=\mathbb{P}(b=0)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{1}\backslash i^{*}}x^{i}\equiv 0\bmod 2\right)+\mathbb{P}(b=1)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{1}\backslash i^{*}}x^{i}\equiv 1\bmod 2\right)=\frac{1}{2},
which reveals that x i ∗ ∼ Bern ⁡ ( 1 / 2 ) x^{i^{*}}\sim\mathrm{Bern}(1/2) and hence ℋ ⁡ ( x i ∗ ) = log ⁡ ( 2 ) {\mathcal{H}}(x^{i^{*}})=\log(2) .
In conclusion, we obtain
∑ i = 1 d ℋ ⁡ ( x i ) = ∑ i ∈ [ d ] \ i ∗ ℋ ⁡ ( x i ) + ℋ ⁡ ( i ∗ ) = d ​ log ⁡ ( 2 ) . \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}(x^{i})=\sum_{i\in[d]\backslash i^{*}}{\mathcal{H}}(x^{i})+{\mathcal{H}}(i^{*})=d\log(2).
(35)
To upper bound ℋ ⁡ ( x ) {\mathcal{H}}(x) , invoke the simple property for entropy function to get
ℋ ⁡ ( x ) ≤ log ⁡ ( | supp ⁡ ( x ) | ) ≤ log ⁡ ( 2 ⋅ 2 | I 1 | − 1 ) = log ⁡ ( 2 ) ​ | I 1 | . \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(x)\leq\log(|\mathrm{supp}(x)|)\leq\log\left(2\cdot 2^{|I_{1}|-1}\right)=\log(2)|I_{1}|.
(36)
The lower bound can be obtained through
ℋ ⁡ ( x ) ≥ ℋ ⁡ ( { x i } i ∈ I 1 \ i ∗ ) = log ⁡ ( 2 | I 1 | − 1 ) = log ⁡ ( 2 ) ​ ( | I 1 | − 1 ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(x)\geq{\mathcal{H}}(\{x^{i}\}_{i\in I_{1}\backslash i^{*}})=\log\left(2^{|I_{1}|-1}\right)=\log(2)(|I_{1}|-1).
(37)
For any i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , when x − i x^{-i} is given, we can recover x i x^{i} by first observing the value of b b from x j x^{j} for any j ∈ I 0 j\in I_{0} , then applying the formula
x i = b + ∑ k ∈ I 1 \ i x k 𝕀 { i ∈ I 1 } mod 2 . x^{i}=b+\sum_{k\in I_{1}\backslash i}x^{k}{\mathbb{I}}\{i\in I_{1}\}\bmod 2.
Thus, x i | x − i x^{i}\mid x^{-i} is always a Dirac measure, which leads to
∑ i = 1 d ℋ ⁡ ( x i ∣ x − i ) = 0 . \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}(x^{i}\mid x^{-i})=0.
(38)
Combining Eqns. ( 35 ), ( 36 ), ( 37 ) and ( 38 ) proves Equation 34 .
Equipped with Equation 34 , we are ready to bound ℬ ⁡ ( p ex ) {\mathcal{B}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}}) and 𝒞 ⁡ ( p ex ) {\mathcal{C}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}}) .
It can easily seen that
ℬ ⁡ ( p ex ) \displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})
= ℋ ⁡ ( x ) − ∑ i = 1 d ℋ ⁡ ( x i ∣ x − i ) ≥ log ⁡ ( 2 ) ​ ( ( 1 − r ) ​ d − 1 ) = Ω ⁡ ( d ) , \displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}(x^{i}\mid x^{-i})\geq\log(2)\big((1-r)d-1\big)=\Omega(d),
𝒞 ⁡ ( p ex ) \displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})
= ∑ i = 1 d ℋ ⁡ ( x i ) − ℋ ⁡ ( x ) ≥ log ⁡ ( 2 ) ​ ( d − | I 1 | ) = log ⁡ ( 2 ) ​ r ​ d = Ω ⁡ ( d ) . \displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}(x^{i})-{\mathcal{H}}(x)\geq\log(2)(d-|I_{1}|)=\log(2)rd=\Omega(d).
For the reverse direction, we can prove the matching lower bound similarly, which leads to
ℬ ⁡ ( p ex ) = Θ ⁡ ( d ) , 𝒞 ⁡ ( p ex ) = Θ ⁡ ( d ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=\Theta(d),\quad{\mathcal{C}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=\Theta(d).
Step 2: Show 𝒟 ⁡ ( p ex ) = O ⁡ ( 1 ) {\mathcal{D}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=O(1) .
Recall the definition of 𝒟 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) {\mathcal{D}}(\cdot) in Eqn. ( 16 ):
𝒟 ⁡ ( p ex ) ≔ ∫ 0 ∞ min ⁡ ( 1 , t ) ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t with ℐ ⁡ ( t ) ≔ ∑ i ≠ j ∈ [ d ] I ⁡ ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) ≥ 0 . \displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\infty}\min(1,t){\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{with}\quad{\mathcal{I}}(t)\coloneqq\sum_{i\neq j\in[d]}\mathrm{I}(x_{t}^{i};x_{t}^{j}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})\geq 0.
To upper bound 𝒟 ⁡ ( p ex ) {\mathcal{D}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}}) , let us write
𝒟 ⁡ ( p ex ) \displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})
= ∫ 0 1 d t ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t + ∫ 1 / d log ⁡ ( d ) min ⁡ { 1 , t } ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t + ∫ log ⁡ ( d ) ∞ ℐ ⁡ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t . \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{d}}t\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\int_{1/d}^{\log(d)}\min\{1,t\}\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\log(d)}^{\infty}{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t.
By direct calculations, one has
∫ 0 1 d t ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{d}}t\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t
≤ 1 d ​ ∫ 0 1 d ℐ ⁡ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t ≤ ℬ ⁡ ( p ex ) d = Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) , \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{d}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{d}}{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{{\mathcal{B}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})}{d}=\Theta(1),
∫ log ⁡ ( d ) ∞ ℐ ⁡ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle\int_{\log(d)}^{\infty}{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t
≤ 1 d − 1 ​ ∫ log ⁡ ( d ) ∞ ( e t − 1 ) ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t ≤ 𝒞 ⁡ ( p ex ) d − 1 = Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{d-1}\int_{\log(d)}^{\infty}(e^{t}-1){\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{{\mathcal{C}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})}{d-1}=\Theta(1).
Therefore, it obeys
𝒟 ⁡ ( p ex ) \displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})
= ∫ 1 / d log ⁡ ( d ) min ⁡ { 1 , t } ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t + O ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle=\int_{1/d}^{\log(d)}\min\{1,t\}\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t+O(1).
To prove 𝒟 ⁡ ( p ex ) = O ⁡ ( 1 ) {\mathcal{D}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=O(1) , it suffices to show that
∫ 1 / d log ⁡ ( d ) min ⁡ { 1 , t } ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t = O ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle\int_{1/d}^{\log(d)}\min\{1,t\}\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t=O(1).
(39)
In view of the definition of ℐ ⁡ ( t ) {\mathcal{I}}(t) , we can decompose it as
ℐ ⁡ ( t ) \displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}(t)
= ( ∑ i , j ∈ I 0 , i ≠ j + ∑ i , j ∈ I 1 , i ≠ j + ∑ i ∈ I 0 , j ∈ I 1 + ∑ i ∈ I 1 , j ∈ I 0 ) I ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) \displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i,j\in I_{0},i\neq j}+\sum_{i,j\in I_{1},i\neq j}+\sum_{i\in I_{0},j\in I_{1}}+\sum_{i\in I_{1},j\in I_{0}}\right)\mathrm{I}(x^{i}_{t};x^{j}_{t}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})
≔ ℐ 1 ​ ( t ) + ℐ 2 ​ ( t ) + ℐ 3 ​ ( t ) + ℐ 4 ​ ( t ) , \displaystyle\coloneqq{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(t)+{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(t)+{\mathcal{I}}_{3}(t)+{\mathcal{I}}_{4}(t),
and we shall bound these four terms separately.
Before diving into the proofs, we make the observation that the mutual information can be computed via
I ⁡ ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) = ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) − ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − i ) . \displaystyle\mathrm{I}(x_{t}^{i};x_{t}^{j}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})={\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})-{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-i}).
(40)
To further compute each entropy terms,
let us introduce two quantities below
ℋ t 1 \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}
= ℋ ⁡ ( e − t ​ δ 0 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK ) = ℋ ⁡ ( e − t ​ δ 1 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK ) = t ​ e − t − log ⁡ ( 1 − e − t ) ​ ( 1 − e − t ) , \displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}(e^{-t}\delta_{0}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}})={\mathcal{H}}(e^{-t}\delta_{1}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}})=te^{-t}-\log(1-e^{-t})(1-e^{-t}),
(41a)
ℋ t 2 \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}
= ℋ ⁡ ( 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 0 + 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 1 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK ) = ( t + log ⁡ ( 2 ) ) ​ e − t − log ⁡ ( 1 − e − t ) ​ ( 1 − e − t ) . \displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{1}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}}\right)=(t+\log(2))e^{-t}-\log(1-e^{-t})(1-e^{-t}).
(41b)
We shall relate our quantities of interest to these terms below.
Case 1: i , j ∈ I 0 i,j\in I_{0} , i ≠ j i\neq j .
For any given x t − ( i , j ) x_{t}^{-(i,j)} , it always holds true that
ℙ ⁡ ( x t i = MASK ) = 1 − e − t , \mathbb{P}(x_{t}^{i}=\mathrm{MASK})=1-e^{-t},
since the noising process is time-homogeneous and independent between coordinates. Recall the definition m ⁡ ( x ) = { i ∈ [ d ] : x i = MASK } m(x)=\{i\in[d]:x^{i}=\mathrm{MASK}\} . Define the event ℰ t , 1 i , j ∈ ℱ t − ( i , j ) {\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i,j}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{-(i,j)} , where ℱ t − ( i , j ) {\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{-(i,j)} is the σ \sigma -algebra generated by x t − ( i , j ) x_{t}^{-(i,j)} , as follows:
ℰ t , 1 i , j ≔ { x t − ( i , j ) : ( ⋁ k ∈ I 0 \ { i , j } { k ∉ m ( x t ) } ) ⋁ ( ⋀ ℓ ∈ I 1 { ℓ ∈ m ( x t ) } ) = 1 } , \displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i,j}\coloneqq\Bigg\{x_{t}^{-(i,j)}:\Bigg(\bigvee_{k\in I_{0}\backslash\{i,j\}}\{k\notin m(x_{t})\}\Bigg)\bigvee\left(\bigwedge_{\ell\in I_{1}}\{\ell\in m(x_{t})\}\right)=1\Bigg\},
where ∧ \wedge is the logical operator AND, and ∨ \vee is the logical operator OR.
By construction of p ex p_{\mathrm{ex}} , it can be checked that
( x t i | x t − ( i , j ) ∈ ℰ t , 1 i , j ) \displaystyle\left(x_{t}^{i}\,\big|\,x_{t}^{-(i,j)}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i,j}\right)
∼ e − t ​ δ 0 / 1 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK ; \displaystyle\sim e^{-t}\delta_{0/1}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}};
( x t i | x t − ( i , j ) ∈ ( ℰ t , 1 i , j ) c ) \displaystyle\left(x_{t}^{i}\,\big|\,x_{t}^{-(i,j)}\in({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i,j})^{c}\right)
∼ 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 0 + 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 1 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK , \displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{1}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}},
where δ 0 / 1 \delta_{0/1} represents either δ 0 \delta_{0} or δ 1 \delta_{1} . Therefore, by the definition of the conditional entropy, we have
ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) = ℋ t 1 ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i , j ) + ℋ t 2 ⋅ ( 1 − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i , j ) ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})={\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}\cdot\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i,j})+{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}\cdot\big(1-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i,j})\big).
(42)
Define the event ℰ t , 1 i ∈ ℱ t − i {\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{-i} , where ℱ t − i {\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{-i} is the σ \sigma -algebra generated by x t − i x_{t}^{-i} , as follows:
ℰ t , 1 i ≔ { x t − i : ( ⋁ k ∈ I 0 \ { i } { k ∉ m ( x t ) } ) ⋁ ( ⋀ ℓ ∈ I 1 { ℓ ∈ m ( x t ) } ) = 1 } . \displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i}\coloneqq\Bigg\{x_{t}^{-i}:\Bigg(\bigvee_{k\in I_{0}\backslash\{i\}}\{k\notin m(x_{t})\}\Bigg)\bigvee\left(\bigwedge_{\ell\in I_{1}}\{\ell\in m(x_{t})\}\right)=1\Bigg\}.
Then, it can be checked similarly that
( x t i | x t − i ∈ ℰ t , 1 i ) \displaystyle\left(x_{t}^{i}\,\big|\,x_{t}^{-i}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i}\right)
∼ e − t ​ δ 0 / 1 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK ; \displaystyle\sim e^{-t}\delta_{0/1}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}};
( x t i | x t − i ∈ ( ℰ t , 1 i ) c ) \displaystyle\left(x_{t}^{i}\,\big|\,x_{t}^{-i}\in({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i})^{c}\right)
∼ 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 0 + 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 1 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK , \displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{1}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}},
which leads to
ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − i ) = ℋ t 1 ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i ) + ℋ t 2 ⋅ ( 1 − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i ) ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-i})={\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}\cdot\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i})+{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}\cdot\big(1-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i})\big).
(43)
Plugging Eqns. ( 42 ) and ( 43 ) into Equation 40 gives that for any i , j ∈ I 0 i,j\in I_{0} , i ≠ j i\neq j ,
I ⁡ ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) \displaystyle\mathrm{I}(x_{t}^{i};x_{t}^{j}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})
= ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) − ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − i ) \displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})-{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-i})
= ℋ t 1 ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i , j ) + ℋ t 2 ⋅ ( 1 − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i , j ) ) − ℋ t 1 ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i ) − ℋ t 2 ⋅ ( 1 − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i ) ) \displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}\cdot\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i,j})+{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}\cdot\big(1-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i,j})\big)-{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}\cdot\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i})-{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}\cdot\big(1-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i})\big)
= ( ℋ t 2 − ℋ t 1 ) ​ ( ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i ) − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 1 i , j ) ) \displaystyle=({\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}-{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1})\big(\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i})-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,1}^{i,j})\big)
= log ⁡ ( 2 ) ​ e − 2 ​ t ​ ( 1 − e − t ) | I 0 | − 2 ​ ( 1 − e − | I 1 | ​ t ) \displaystyle=\log(2)e^{-2t}\left(1-e^{-t}\right)^{|I_{0}|-2}\left(1-e^{-|I_{1}|t}\right)
= O ⁡ ( e − 2 ​ t ​ ( 1 − e − t ) r ​ d / 2 ) , \displaystyle=O\left(e^{-2t}(1-e^{-t})^{rd/2}\right),
whose value is independent of the indices i i and j j .
Since | { i , j ∈ I 0 : i ≠ j } | = r d ( r d − 1 ) = Θ ( d 2 ) |\{i,j\in I_{0}:i\neq j\}|=rd(rd-1)=\Theta(d^{2}) , quantity ℐ 1 ​ ( t ) {\mathcal{I}}_{1}(t) satisfies
ℐ 1 ​ ( t ) = ∑ i , j ∈ I 0 , i ≠ j I ⁡ ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) = O ⁡ ( d 2 ​ e − 2 ​ t ​ ( 1 − e − t ) r ​ d / 2 ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(t)=\sum_{i,j\in I_{0},i\neq j}\mathrm{I}(x_{t}^{i};x_{t}^{j}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})=O\left(d^{2}e^{-2t}(1-e^{-t})^{rd/2}\right).
(44)
Case 2: i , j ∈ I 1 i,j\in I_{1} , i ≠ j i\neq j .
Following the proof strategy in Case 1, for any given x t − ( i , j ) x_{t}^{-(i,j)} , it holds that
x t i | x t − ( i , j ) ∼ 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 0 + 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 1 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK , \displaystyle x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)}\sim\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{1}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}},
which implies that
ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) = ℋ t 2 . \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})={\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}.
(45)
Define the event ℰ t , 2 i ∈ ℱ t − i {\mathcal{E}}_{t,2}^{i}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{-i} as follows:
ℰ t , 2 i ≔ { x t − i : ( ⋁ k ∈ I 0 { k ∉ m ( x t ) } ) ⋀ ( ⋀ ℓ ∈ I 1 \ { i } { ℓ ∈ m ( x t ) } ) = 1 } , \displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{t,2}^{i}\coloneqq\Bigg\{x_{t}^{-i}:\Bigg(\bigvee_{k\in I_{0}}\{k\notin m(x_{t})\}\Bigg)\bigwedge\Bigg(\bigwedge_{\ell\in I_{1}\backslash\{i\}}\{\ell\in m(x_{t})\}\Bigg)=1\Bigg\},
which induces
( x t i | x t − i ∈ ℰ t , 2 i ) \displaystyle\left(x_{t}^{i}\,\big|\,x_{t}^{-i}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{t,2}^{i}\right)
∼ e − t ​ δ 0 / 1 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK ; \displaystyle\sim e^{-t}\delta_{0/1}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}};
( x t i | x t − i ∈ ( ℰ t , 2 i ) c ) \displaystyle\left(x_{t}^{i}\,\big|\,x_{t}^{-i}\in({\mathcal{E}}_{t,2}^{i})^{c}\right)
∼ 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 0 + 1 2 ​ e − t ​ δ 1 + ( 1 − e − t ) ​ δ MASK , \displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-t}\delta_{1}+(1-e^{-t})\delta_{\mathrm{MASK}},
and the conditional entropy formula
ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − i ) = ℋ t 1 ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 2 i ) + ℋ t 2 ⋅ ( 1 − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 2 i ) ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-i})={\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}\cdot\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,2}^{i})+{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}\cdot\big(1-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,2}^{i})\big).
(46)
Plugging Eqns. ( 45 ) and ( 46 ) into Equation 40 gives that for any i , j ∈ I 0 i,j\in I_{0} , i ≠ j i\neq j ,
I ⁡ ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) \displaystyle\mathrm{I}(x_{t}^{i};x_{t}^{j}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})
= ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) − ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − i ) \displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})-{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-i})
= ℋ t 2 − ℋ t 1 ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 2 i ) − ℋ t 2 ⋅ ( 1 − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 2 i ) ) \displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}-{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}\cdot\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,2}^{i})-{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}\cdot\big(1-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,2}^{i})\big)
= ( ℋ t 2 − ℋ t 1 ) ​ ℙ ​ ( ℰ t , 2 i ) = O ⁡ ( e − ( 1 − r ) ​ d ​ t ) , \displaystyle=\left({\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}-{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}\right)\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,2}^{i})=O\left(e^{-(1-r)dt}\right),
whose value is, again, independent of the indices i i and j j . Since | { i , j ∈ I 1 : i ≠ j } | = ( 1 − r ) d ( ( 1 − r ) d − 1 ) = Θ ( d 2 ) |\{i,j\in I_{1}:i\neq j\}|=(1-r)d((1-r)d-1)=\Theta(d^{2}) , we reach
ℐ 2 ​ ( t ) = ∑ i , j ∈ I 1 , i ≠ j I ⁡ ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) = O ⁡ ( d 2 ​ e − ( 1 − r ) ​ d ​ t ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(t)=\sum_{i,j\in I_{1},i\neq j}\mathrm{I}(x_{t}^{i};x_{t}^{j}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})=O(d^{2}e^{-(1-r)dt}).
(47)
Case 3: i ∈ I 0 i\in I_{0} , j ∈ I 1 j\in I_{1} .
Define the function ℋ B ​ ( p ) ≔ − p ​ log ⁡ ( p ) − ( 1 − p ) ​ log ⁡ ( 1 − p ) {\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(p)\coloneqq-p\log(p)-(1-p)\log(1-p) to be the entropy of the distribution Bern ⁡ ( p ) \mathrm{Bern}(p) .
Following the proofs of the two cases above, let us define events
ℰ t , 3 i , j \displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{t,3}^{i,j}
≔ { x t − ( i , j ) : ( ⋁ k ∈ I 0 \ { i } { k ≠ m ( x t ) } ) ⋁ ( ⋀ ℓ ∈ I 1 \ { j } { ℓ ∈ m ( x t ) } ) = 1 } ; \displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigg\{x_{t}^{-(i,j)}:\Bigg(\bigvee_{k\in I_{0}\backslash\{i\}}\{k\neq m(x_{t})\}\Bigg)\bigvee\Bigg(\bigwedge_{\ell\in I_{1}\backslash\{j\}}\{\ell\in m(x_{t})\}\Bigg)=1\Bigg\};
ℰ t , 3 i \displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{t,3}^{i}
≔ { x t − i : ( ⋁ k ∈ I 0 \ { i } { k ≠ m ( x t ) } ) ⋁ ( ⋀ ℓ ∈ I 1 { ℓ ∈ m ( x t ) } ) = 1 } . \displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigg\{x_{t}^{-i}:\Bigg(\bigvee_{k\in I_{0}\backslash\{i\}}\{k\neq m(x_{t})\}\Bigg)\bigvee\left(\bigwedge_{\ell\in I_{1}}\{\ell\in m(x_{t})\}\right)=1\Bigg\}.
Similar calculations yield
ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) = ℋ t 1 ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 3 i , j ) + ℋ t 2 ⋅ ( 1 − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 3 i , j ) ) ; \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})={\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}\cdot\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,3}^{i,j})+{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}\cdot\big(1-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,3}^{i,j})\big);
ℋ ⁡ ( x t i ∣ x t − i ) = ℋ t 1 ⋅ ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 3 i ) + ℋ t 2 ⋅ ( 1 − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 3 i ) ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t}^{i}\mid x_{t}^{-i})={\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1}\cdot\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,3}^{i})+{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}\cdot\big(1-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,3}^{i})\big).
Therefore, we obtain
I ⁡ ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) \displaystyle\mathrm{I}(x_{t}^{i};x_{t}^{j}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})
= ( ℋ t 2 − ℋ t 1 ) ​ ( ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 3 i ) − ℙ ⁡ ( ℰ t , 3 i , j ) ) \displaystyle=({\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{2}-{\mathcal{H}}_{t}^{1})\big(\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,3}^{i})-\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{t,3}^{i,j})\big)
= log ⁡ ( 2 ) ​ e − | I 1 | ​ t ​ ( 1 − e − t ) | I 0 | = O ⁡ ( e − ℋ B ​ ( r ) ​ d ) , \displaystyle=\log(2)e^{-|I_{1}|t}\left(1-e^{-t}\right)^{|I_{0}|}=O\left(e^{-{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)d}\right),
where the last equality is due to the fact that e − | I 1 | ​ t ​ ( 1 − e − t ) | I 0 | e^{-|I_{1}|t}\left(1-e^{-t}\right)^{|I_{0}|} is maximized at t = − log ⁡ ( 1 − r ) t=-\log(1-r) .
Finally, with | { i ∈ I 0 , j ∈ I 1 } | = r ⁡ ( 1 − r ) ​ d 2 |\{i\in I_{0},j\in I_{1}\}|=r(1-r)d^{2} , we can bound
ℐ 3 ​ ( t ) = ∑ i ∈ I 0 , j ∈ I 1 I ⁡ ( x t i ; x t j ∣ x t − ( i , j ) ) = O ⁡ ( d 2 ​ e − ℋ B ​ ( r ) ​ d ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{3}(t)=\sum_{i\in I_{0},j\in I_{1}}I(x_{t}^{i};x_{t}^{j}\mid x_{t}^{-(i,j)})=O\left(d^{2}e^{-{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)d}\right).
(48)
Case 4: i ∈ I 1 i\in I_{1} , j ∈ I 0 j\in I_{0} .
Notice that ℐ 3 ​ ( t ) {\mathcal{I}}_{3}(t) and ℐ 4 ​ ( t ) {\mathcal{I}}_{4}(t) are invariant under swapping i i and j j . We can show in the same way as above that
ℐ 4 ​ ( t ) = O ⁡ ( d 2 ​ e − ℋ B ​ ( r ) ​ d ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{4}(t)=O\left(d^{2}e^{-{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)d}\right).
(49)
Putting everything together.
Combining Eqns. ( 44 ), ( 47 ), ( 48 ) and ( 49 ), we arrive at
ℐ ⁡ ( t ) ≲ d 2 ​ ( e − 2 ​ t ​ ( 1 − e − t ) r ​ d / 2 + e − ( 1 − r ) ​ d ​ t + e − ℋ B ​ ( r ) ​ d ) . \displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}(t)\lesssim d^{2}\left(e^{-2t}(1-e^{-t})^{rd/2}+e^{-(1-r)dt}+e^{-{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)d}\right).
(50)
We are now in a position to prove Equation 39 . Let us begin with the integration over the time interval t ∈ [ 1 / d , 1 ] t\in[1/d,1] .
Direct calculation yields that e − 2 ​ t ​ ( 1 − e − t ) r ​ d / 2 e^{-2t}(1-e^{-t})^{rd/2} is maximized at t ∗ = log ⁡ ( 1 + r ​ d 4 ) > 1 t^{*}=\log(1+\frac{rd}{4})>1 , which reveals that
d 2 ​ e − 2 ​ t ​ ( 1 − e − t ) r ​ d / 2 ≤ d 2 ​ e − 2 ​ t ∗ = d 2 ​ ( 1 + r ​ d 4 ) − 2 = O ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle d^{2}e^{-2t}(1-e^{-t})^{rd/2}\leq d^{2}e^{-2t^{*}}=d^{2}\left(1+\frac{rd}{4}\right)^{-2}=O(1).
(51)
For the term from ℐ 2 ​ ( t ) {\mathcal{I}}_{2}(t) , we obtain
∫ 1 d 1 t ⋅ d 2 e − ( 1 − r ) ​ d ​ t d t = ( a ) ∫ 1 d s e − ( 1 − r ) ​ s d s ≤ ∫ 0 ∞ s e − ( 1 − r ) ​ s d s = 1 ( 1 − r ) 2 = O ( 1 ) , \displaystyle\int_{\frac{1}{d}}^{1}t\cdot d^{2}e^{-(1-r)dt}\mathrm{d}t\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{=}\int_{1}^{d}se^{-(1-r)s}\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}se^{-(1-r)s}\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{(1-r)^{2}}=O(1),
(52)
where in (a), we use the change of variable formula with s = d ​ t s=dt .
Similarly, we can show that
∫ 1 d 1 t ⋅ d 2 ​ e − ℋ B ​ ( r ) ​ d ​ 𝑑 t ≤ ∫ 0 1 t ⋅ d 2 ​ e − ℋ B ​ ( r ) ​ d ​ 𝑑 t = 1 2 ​ d 2 ​ e − ℋ B ​ ( r ) ​ d = O ⁡ ( 1 ) , \displaystyle\int_{\frac{1}{d}}^{1}t\cdot d^{2}e^{-{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)d}\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{1}t\cdot d^{2}e^{-{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)d}\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{2}d^{2}e^{-{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)d}=O(1),
(53)
where the condition min ⁡ { r , 1 − r } = Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) \min\{r,1-r\}=\Theta(1) ensures ℋ B ​ ( r ) = Θ ​ ( 1 ) {\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)=\Theta(1) .
Taking collectively Eqns. ( 50 ), ( 51 ), ( 52 ) and ( 53 ), we arrive at
∫ 1 d 1 min ⁡ { 1 , t } ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t = ∫ 1 d 1 t ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t = O ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle\int_{\frac{1}{d}}^{1}\min\{1,t\}\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\frac{1}{d}}^{1}t\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t=O(1).
(54)
Let us move on to the integration over time interval t ∈ [ 1 , log ⁡ ( d ) ] t\in[1,\log(d)] . The integral computation yields that
∫ 1 log ⁡ ( d ) d 2 e − 2 ​ t ( 1 − e − t ) r ​ d / 2 d t = ( a ) ∫ 1 d / e s ( 1 − s d ) r ​ d / 2 d s ≤ ( b ) ∫ 0 ∞ s e − r s / 2 d s = O ( 1 ) , \displaystyle\int_{1}^{\log(d)}d^{2}e^{-2t}(1-e^{-t})^{rd/2}\mathrm{d}t\overset{(a)}{=}\int_{1}^{d/e}s\left(1-\frac{s}{d}\right)^{rd/2}\mathrm{d}s\overset{(b)}{\leq}\int_{0}^{\infty}se^{-rs/2}\mathrm{d}s=O(1),
(55)
where in (a), we use the change of variable formula with s = d ​ e − t s=de^{-t} , and in (b), we use the inequality ( 1 − x ) 1 / x ≤ e − 1 (1-x)^{1/x}\leq e^{-1} for x ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] x\in(0,1] .
For the remaining terms, we have
∫ 1 log ⁡ ( d ) d 2 ​ e − ( 1 − r ) ​ d ​ t ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle\int_{1}^{\log(d)}d^{2}e^{-(1-r)dt}\mathrm{d}t
≤ ∫ 0 log ⁡ ( d ) d 2 ​ e − ( 1 − r ) ​ d ​ 𝑑 t = d 2 ​ log ⁡ ( d ) ​ e − ( 1 − r ) ​ d = O ⁡ ( 1 ) ; \displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\log(d)}d^{2}e^{-(1-r)d}\mathrm{d}t=d^{2}\log(d)e^{-(1-r)d}=O(1);
(56)
∫ 1 log ⁡ ( d ) d 2 ​ e − ℋ B ​ ( r ) ​ d ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle\int_{1}^{\log(d)}d^{2}e^{-{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)d}\mathrm{d}t
≤ d 2 ​ log ⁡ ( d ) ​ e − ℋ B ​ ( r ) ​ d = O ⁡ ( 1 ) , \displaystyle\leq d^{2}\log(d)e^{-{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)d}=O(1),
(57)
where the condition min ⁡ { r , 1 − r } = Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) \min\{r,1-r\}=\Theta(1) ensures ℋ B ​ ( r ) = Θ ​ ( 1 ) {\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{B}}(r)=\Theta(1) .
Now, combining Eqns. ( 50 ), ( 55 ), ( 56 ) and ( 57 ) yields
∫ 1 log ⁡ ( d ) min ⁡ { 1 , t } ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t = ∫ 1 log ⁡ ( d ) ℐ ⁡ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t = O ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle\int_{1}^{\log(d)}\min\{1,t\}\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\int_{1}^{\log(d)}{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t=O(1).
(58)
Finally, equipped with Eqns. ( 54 ) and ( 58 ), we conclude
∫ 1 d log ⁡ ( d ) min ⁡ { 1 , t } ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t = ∫ 1 d 1 min ⁡ { 1 , t } ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t + ∫ 1 log ⁡ ( d ) min ⁡ { 1 , t } ​ ℐ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t = O ⁡ ( 1 ) , \displaystyle\int_{\frac{1}{d}}^{\log(d)}\min\{1,t\}\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\frac{1}{d}}^{1}\min\{1,t\}\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{\log(d)}\min\{1,t\}\,{\mathcal{I}}(t)\mathrm{d}t=O(1),
which proves 𝒟 ⁡ ( p ex ) = O ⁡ ( 1 ) {\mathcal{D}}(p_{\mathrm{ex}})=O(1) .
∎
Appendix B Technical preparations
B.1 Score functions
Below, we present an equivalent formulation of the score functions.
Proposition 6 .
Let q 0 q_{0} be an initial distribution on 𝒳 0 {\mathcal{X}}_{0} . Let x , y ∈ 𝒳 x,y\in{\mathcal{X}} be such that Q ⁡ ( y , x ) > 0 Q(y,x)>0 . Then,
1.
for the uniform noising process,
s t ​ ( y , x ) = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 ​ α t d_H ⁡ ( y , x 0 ) 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 ​ α t d_H ⁡ ( x , x 0 ) , s_{t}(y,x)=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\alpha_{t}^{\ham(y,x_{0})}}{{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\alpha_{t}^{\ham{(x,x_{0})}}},
(59)
where α t ≔ 1 − e − t 1 + ( S − 1 ) ​ e − t \alpha_{t}\coloneqq\frac{1-e^{-t}}{1+(S-1)e^{-t}} .
2.
for the masking noising process,
s t ​ ( y , x ) = 1 e t − 1 ​ q 0 ​ ( y ) q 0 ​ ( x ) , s_{t}(y,x)=\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}\frac{q_{0}(y)}{q_{0}(x)},
(60)
where for x ∈ 𝒳 ∖ 𝒳 0 x\in{\mathcal{X}}\setminus{\mathcal{X}}_{0} , q 0 ​ ( x ) q_{0}(x) is the marginal probability of the unmasked coordinates of x x under q 0 q_{0} .
Proof of Proposition 6 . By the definition of the score function, one can write
s t ​ ( y , x ) = q t ​ ( y ) q t ​ ( x ) = ∑ x 0 q t | 0 ​ ( y ∣ x 0 ) ​ q 0 ​ ( x 0 ) ∑ x 0 q t | 0 ​ ( x ∣ x 0 ) ​ q 0 ​ ( x 0 ) . \displaystyle s_{t}(y,x)=\frac{q_{t}(y)}{q_{t}(x)}=\frac{\sum_{x_{0}}q_{t\mid 0}(y\mid x_{0})q_{0}(x_{0})}{\sum_{x_{0}}q_{t\mid 0}(x\mid x_{0})q_{0}(x_{0})}.
For the uniform noising process, one can solve the Kolmogorov forward equation for every dimension. As a result, the transition can be written as
q t | 0 ​ ( y ∣ x 0 ) = ( 1 − e − t S ) d_H ⁡ ( y , x 0 ) ​ ( 1 + ( S − 1 ) ​ e − t S ) d − d_H ⁡ ( y , x 0 ) = ( 1 + ( S − 1 ) ​ e − t S ) d ​ α t d_H ⁡ ( y , x 0 ) , \displaystyle q_{t\mid 0}(y\mid x_{0})=\left(\frac{1-e^{-t}}{S}\right)^{\ham(y,x_{0})}\left(\frac{1+(S-1)e^{-t}}{S}\right)^{d-\ham(y,x_{0})}=\left(\frac{1+(S-1)e^{-t}}{S}\right)^{d}\alpha_{t}^{\ham(y,x_{0})},
which proves Equation 59 . More details of this relation can be found in (e.g., Zhang et al. (2025) , Proposition 1).
For the masking noising process, for notational convenience, given any x ∈ ( [ S ] ∪ { MASK } ) d x\in([S]\cup\{\mathrm{MASK}\})^{d} , define
m ⁡ ( x ) ≔ { i ∈ [ d ] : x i = MASK } . \displaystyle m(x)\coloneqq\{i\in[d]:x^{i}=\mathrm{MASK}\}.
(61)
In view of this piece of notation, as Pr ⁡ ( x t i = MASK ) = e − t \Pr(x_{t}^{i}=\mathrm{MASK})=e^{-t} , and coordinates evolve independently, one can write
q t | 0 ​ ( y ∣ x 0 ) = ( 1 − e − t ) | m ⁡ ( y ) | ​ e − t ⁡ ( d − | m ⁡ ( y ) | ) ​ 𝕀 ​ { for all ​ i ∈ [ d ] , y i ∈ { x 0 i , MASK } } . \displaystyle q_{t\mid 0}(y\mid x_{0})=(1-e^{-t})^{\lvert m(y)\rvert}e^{-t(d-\lvert m(y)\rvert)}{\mathbb{I}}\{\,\text{for all }i\in[d],y^{i}\in\{x_{0}^{i},\mathrm{MASK}\}\,\}.
As Q ⁡ ( y , x ) > 0 Q(y,x)>0 , it must be that d_H ⁡ ( x , y ) = 1 \ham(x,y)=1 , and for i i , such that x i ≠ y i x^{i}\neq y^{i} , x i = MASK x^{i}=\mathrm{MASK} and y i ≠ MASK y^{i}\neq\mathrm{MASK} .
This implies that | m ⁡ ( x ) | = | m ⁡ ( y ) | + 1 \lvert m(x)\rvert=\lvert m(y)\rvert+1 , and we can write
∑ x 0 q t | 0 ​ ( y ∣ x 0 ) ​ q 0 ​ ( x 0 ) ∑ x 0 q t | 0 ​ ( x ∣ x 0 ) ​ q 0 ​ ( x 0 ) = e − t 1 − e − t ​ ∑ x 0 q 0 ​ ( x 0 ) ​ 𝕀 ​ { for all ​ i ∈ [ d ] , y i ∈ { x 0 i , MASK } } ∑ x 0 q 0 ​ ( x 0 ) ​ 𝕀 ​ { for all ​ i ∈ [ d ] , x i ∈ { x 0 i , MASK } } = 1 e t − 1 ​ q 0 ​ ( y ) q 0 ​ ( x ) . \displaystyle\frac{\sum_{x_{0}}q_{t\mid 0}(y\mid x_{0})q_{0}(x_{0})}{\sum_{x_{0}}q_{t\mid 0}(x\mid x_{0})q_{0}(x_{0})}=\frac{e^{-t}}{1-e^{-t}}\frac{\sum_{x_{0}}q_{0}(x_{0}){\mathbb{I}}\{\,\text{for all }i\in[d],y^{i}\in\{x_{0}^{i},\mathrm{MASK}\}\,\}}{\sum_{x_{0}}q_{0}(x_{0}){\mathbb{I}}\{\,\text{for all }i\in[d],x^{i}\in\{x_{0}^{i},\mathrm{MASK}\}\,\}}=\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}\frac{q_{0}(y)}{q_{0}(x)}.
∎
B.2 Technical lemmas
Lemma 4 (Chain rule of KL divergence) .
For N > 0 N>0 , let a 0 : N , b 0 : N a_{0:N},b_{0:N} be the joint distributions of two Markov processes. Then,
𝖪𝖫 ( a 0 : N ∥ b 0 : N ) = 𝖪𝖫 ( a 0 ∥ b 0 ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 𝔼 x ∼ a k 𝖪𝖫 ( a k + 1 | k ( ⋅ ∣ x ) ∥ b k + 1 | k ( ⋅ ∣ x ) ) . \mathsf{KL}(a_{0:N}\|b_{0:N})=\mathsf{KL}(a_{0}\|b_{0})+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim a_{k}}\mathsf{KL}\left(a_{k+1\mid k}(\cdot\mid x)\|b_{k+1\mid k}(\cdot\mid x)\right).
Proof of Lemma 4 . Invoking the definition of KL divergence with some direct calculations yields
𝖪𝖫 ( a 0 : N ∥ b 0 : N ) \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(a_{0:N}\|b_{0:N})
= 𝔼 x 0 : N ∼ a 0 : N log a 0 : N ( x 0 : N ) b 0 : N ( x 0 : N ) \displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x_{0:N}\sim a_{0:N}}\log\frac{a_{0:N}(x_{0:N})}{b_{0:N}(x_{0:N})}
= 𝔼 x 0 : N ∼ a 0 : N log ( a 0 ​ ( x 0 ) b 0 ​ ( x 0 ) ∏ k = 0 N − 1 a k + 1 | k ​ ( x k + 1 ∣ x k ) b k + 1 | k ​ ( x k + 1 ∣ x k ) ) \displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x_{0:N}\sim a_{0:N}}\log\left(\frac{a_{0}(x_{0})}{b_{0}(x_{0})}\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\frac{a_{k+1\mid k}(x_{k+1}\mid x_{k})}{b_{k+1\mid k}(x_{k+1}\mid x_{k})}\right)
= 𝔼 x 0 ∼ a 0 log a 0 ​ ( x 0 ) b 0 ​ ( x 0 ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 𝔼 x k ∼ a k 𝔼 x k + 1 ∼ a k + 1 | k ( ⋅ ∣ x k ) log a k + 1 | k ​ ( x k + 1 ∣ x k ) b k + 1 | k ​ ( x k + 1 ∣ x k ) \displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x_{0}\sim a_{0}}\log\frac{a_{0}(x_{0})}{b_{0}(x_{0})}+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{k}\sim a_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{k+1}\sim a_{k+1\mid k}(\cdot\mid x_{k})}\log\frac{a_{k+1\mid k}(x_{k+1}\mid x_{k})}{b_{k+1\mid k}(x_{k+1}\mid x_{k})}
= 𝖪𝖫 ( a 0 ∥ b 0 ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 𝔼 x k ∼ a k 𝖪𝖫 ( a k + 1 | k ( ⋅ ∣ x k ) ∥ b k + 1 | k ( ⋅ ∣ x k ) ) . \displaystyle=\mathsf{KL}(a_{0}\|b_{0})+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{k}\sim a_{k}}\mathsf{KL}\left(a_{k+1\mid k}(\cdot\mid x_{k})\|b_{k+1\mid k}(\cdot\mid x_{k})\right).
∎
Lemma 5 (Derivative of KL: an upper bound) .
Let ( q t ) (q_{t}) and ( p t ) (p_{t}) be the marginals of CTMCs with rate matrices ( Q t ) (Q_{t}) and ( Q ^ t ) (\widehat{Q}_{t}) , respectively, and q ← t ≡ q T − t \overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t}\equiv q_{T-t} be the marginals of the reverse process. Then, for any t > t k t>t_{k} and for any z z ,
∂ ∂ t 𝖪𝖫 ( q ← t | t k ( ⋅ ∣ z ) ∥ p t | t k ( ⋅ ∣ z ) ) \displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathsf{KL}\left(\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(\cdot\mid z)\|p_{t\mid t_{k}}(\cdot\mid z)\right)
≤ 𝔼 x t ∼ q ← t | t k ( ⋅ ∣ z ) ∑ y ≠ x t [ Q ^ t ( x t , y ) − Q ← t ( x t , y ) + Q ← t ( x t , y ) log Q ← t ​ ( x t , y ) Q ^ t ​ ( x t , y ) ] . \displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{t}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(\cdot\mid z)}\sum_{y\neq x_{t}}\left[\widehat{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)-\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)+\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)\log\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)}{\widehat{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)}\right].
Proof of Lemma 5 .
Let us omit the conditioning on z z for the notation brevity.
By direct calculations, one can write
A \displaystyle A
≔ ∂ ∂ t 𝖪𝖫 ( q ← t | t k ∥ p t | t k ) = ∑ x ∈ 𝒳 ( ∂ ∂ t q ← t | t k ( x ) ) log q ← t | t k ​ ( x ) p t | t k ​ ( x ) − ∑ x ∈ 𝒳 q ← t | t k ( x ) ∂ ∂ t ​ p t | t k ​ ( x ) p t | t k ​ ( x ) . \displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathsf{KL}\left(\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}\|p_{t\mid t_{k}}\right)=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)\right)\log\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}-\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}.
Recall the Kolmogorov equation:
∂ ∂ t ​ q ← t | t k ​ ( x ) = ∑ y ∈ 𝒳 Q ← ​ ( y , x ) ​ q ← t | t k ​ ( y ) and ∂ ∂ t ​ p t | t k ​ ( x ) = ∑ y ∈ 𝒳 Q ^ ​ ( y , x ) ​ p t | t k ​ ( y ) \frac{\partial}{\partial t}\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)=\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}(y,x)\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)=\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\widehat{Q}(y,x)p_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)
Putting the above together, we obtain (relabeling x x and y y )

= 𝔼 x ∼ q ← t | t k ​ ∑ y ∈ 𝒳 [ Q ← ​ ( x , y ) ​ log ⁡ ( q ← t | t k ​ ( y ) p t | t k ​ ( y ) ) − Q ^ ​ ( x , y ) ​ q ← t | t k ​ ( y ) q ← t | t k ​ ( x ) ⋅ p t | t k ​ ( x ) p t | t k ​ ( y ) ] \displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}}\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\left[\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}(x,y)\log\left(\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}\right)-\widehat{Q}(x,y)\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}{\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}\cdot\frac{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}\right]
(62)
= 𝔼 x ∼ q ← t | t k ​ ∑ y ≠ x [ Q ← ​ ( x , y ) ​ log ⁡ ( q ← t | t k ​ ( y ) p t | t k ​ ( y ) ) − Q ← ​ ( x , y ) ​ log ⁡ ( q ← t | t k ​ ( x ) p t | t k ​ ( x ) ) − Q ^ ​ ( x , y ) ​ q ← t | t k ​ ( y ) q ← t | t k ​ ( x ) ⋅ p t | t k ​ ( x ) p t | t k ​ ( y ) ] − Q ^ ​ ( x , x ) \displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}}\sum_{y\neq x}\left[\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}(x,y)\log\left(\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}\right)-\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}(x,y)\log\left(\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}\right)-\widehat{Q}(x,y)\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}{\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}\cdot\frac{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}\right]-\widehat{Q}(x,x)

it satisfies that

≤ 𝔼 x ∼ q ← t | t k ∑ y ≠ x [ Q ^ ( x , y ) + Q ← ( x , y ) log ( Q ← t ​ ( x , y ) Q ^ t ​ ( x , y ) ) − Q ← ( x , y ) \displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}}\sum_{y\neq x}\Biggl[\widehat{Q}(x,y)+\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}(x,y)\log\left(\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x,y)}{\widehat{Q}_{t}(x,y)}\right)-\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}(x,y)
+ Q ← t ( x , y ) Q ^ t ​ ( x , y ) C x ​ y ⋅ p t | t k ​ ( x ) p t | t k ​ ( y ) − Q ^ ( x , y ) Q ← t ​ ( x , y ) C x ​ y ⋅ p t | t k ​ ( x ) p t | t k ​ ( y ) ] \displaystyle+\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x,y)\frac{\widehat{Q}_{t}(x,y)}{C_{xy}}\cdot\frac{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}-\widehat{Q}(x,y)\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x,y)}{C_{xy}}\cdot\frac{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(x)}{p_{t\mid t_{k}}(y)}\Biggr]
= 𝔼 x ∼ q ← t | t k ​ ∑ y ≠ x [ Q ^ t ​ ( x , y ) − Q ← t ​ ( x , y ) + Q ← t ​ ( x , y ) ​ log ⁡ ( Q ← t ​ ( x , y ) Q ^ t ​ ( x , y ) ) ] . \displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}}\sum_{y\neq x}\left[\widehat{Q}_{t}(x,y)-\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x,y)+\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x,y)\log\left(\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x,y)}{\widehat{Q}_{t}(x,y)}\right)\right].
∎
Lemma 6 (Itô’s Lemma for Poisson jump process) .
For the Poisson jump process { x t } t ≥ 0 \{x_{t}\}_{t\geq 0} with generator { L t } t ≥ 0 \{L_{t}\}_{t\geq 0} and rate matrix { R t } t ≥ 0 \{R_{t}\}_{t\geq 0} . Itô’s Lemma formula can be written as
f ⁡ ( t , x t ) = f ⁡ ( 0 , x 0 ) + ∫ 0 t [ ∂ s f ⁡ ( s , x s − ) + ( L s ​ f ) ​ ( s , x s − ) ] ​ 𝑑 t + M t , \displaystyle f(t,x_{t})=f(0,x_{0})+\int_{0}^{t}\big[\partial_{s}f(s,x_{s^{-}})+\left(L_{s}f\right)(s,x_{s^{-}})\big]\mathrm{d}t+M_{t},
(65)
where x s − = lim u → s − x s x_{s-}=\lim_{u\to s^{-}}x_{s} , which exists for almost everywhere s ∈ [ 0 , t ) s\in[0,t) under the Lebesgue measure. The compensation process { M u } u ∈ [ ℓ , t ] \{M_{u}\}_{u\in[\ell,t]} is defined as
M u = ∑ y s : y s ≠ x s ∫ ℓ u ( f ( s , y s ) − f ( s , x s ) ) ( d N s x s , y s − λ s x s , y s d s ) , \displaystyle M_{u}=\sum_{y_{s}:y_{s}\neq x_{s}}\int_{\ell}^{u}\big(f(s,y_{s})-f(s,x_{s})\big)\big(\mathrm{d}N_{s}^{x_{s},y_{s}}-\lambda_{s}^{x_{s},y_{s}}\mathrm{d}s\big),
where N s x , y N_{s}^{x,y} is the counting process of jumps from x x to y y up to time t t and λ s x , y \lambda_{s}^{x,y} is the ℱ s − {\mathcal{F}}_{s^{-}} -intensity of N s x , y N_{s}^{x,y} , i.e., λ s x , y = 𝕀 { x s − = y } R t ( x , y ) \lambda_{s}^{x,y}={\mathbb{I}}\{x_{s^{-}}=y\}R_{t}(x,y) .
See ( Conforti et al., 2025 , Appendix A.5) for more details.
Appendix C Proofs of results in Section 3.1
C.1 Proof of Theorem 1
We first decompose the KL divergence between the output distribution p T p_{T} and the target distribution q 0 q_{0} as
𝖪𝖫 ( q 0 ∥ p T ) \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(q_{0}\|p_{T})
≤ 𝖪𝖫 ( q T − t 0 , … , T − t N ∥ p t 0 , … , t N ) \displaystyle\leq\mathsf{KL}(q_{T-t_{0},\ldots,T-t_{N}}\|p_{t_{0},\ldots,t_{N}})
= 𝖪𝖫 ( q T ∥ p 0 ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 𝔼 x t k ∼ q ← t k [ 𝖪𝖫 ( q ← t k + 1 | t k ( ⋅ | x t k ) ∥ p t k + 1 | t k ( ⋅ | x t k ) ) ] , \displaystyle=\mathsf{KL}(q_{T}\|p_{0})+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{t_{k}}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t_{k}}}\left[\mathsf{KL}\left(\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t_{k+1}\mid t_{k}}(\cdot|x_{t_{k}})\|p_{t_{k+1}\mid t_{k}}(\cdot|x_{t_{k}})\right)\right],
(66)
where the first inequality follows from the data-processing inequality for KL divergence and the second inequality follows from the chain rule for KL divergence in Lemma 4 .
The first term is the initialization error, which can be upper bounded by the following lemma.
Lemma 7 .
For the uniform noising process, for any initial distribution q 0 ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) q_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{X}}) , time index t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , one has the same limit distribution
q t ​ → 𝑑 ​ p 0 = Unif ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) , as ​ t → ∞ . \displaystyle q_{t}\overset{d}{\to}p_{0}=\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{X}}),\quad\text{as }t\to\infty.
Further, the modified log-Sobolev constant 3 3
3
C LSI C_{\mathrm{LSI}} is defined as the smallest number such that for any q ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) q\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{X}}) , 𝖪𝖫 ⁡ ( q ∣ Unif ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) ) ≤ C LSI / 2 ⋅ ℰ ⁡ ( q , log ⁡ ( q ) ) \mathsf{KL}(q\mid\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{X}}))\leq C_{\mathrm{LSI}}/2\cdot{\mathcal{E}}(q,\log(q)) , where ℰ {\mathcal{E}} is the Dirichlet form associated with the uniform noising process, i.e., ℰ ( f , g ) = − ( 2 | 𝒳 | ) − 1 ∑ x , y ∈ 𝒳 ( f ( x ) − f ( y ) ) ( g ( x ) − g ( y ) ) Q ( x , y ) {\mathcal{E}}(f,g)=-(2|{\mathcal{X}}|)^{-1}\sum_{x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}}(f(x)-f(y))(g(x)-g(y))Q(x,y) . of q t q_{t} satisfies C LSI = 2 C_{\mathrm{LSI}}=2 , which leads to
𝖪𝖫 ( q t ∥ p 0 ) ≤ e − t 𝖪𝖫 ( q 0 ∥ p 0 ) ≤ e − t d log ( S ) . \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(q_{t}\|p_{0})\leq e^{-t}\mathsf{KL}(q_{0}\|p_{0})\leq e^{-t}d\log(S).
The proof of Lemma 7 can be found in previous works, e.g., Zhang et al. (2025, Proposition 2) . Applying the lemma above together with Lemma 5 and Equation 7 on the second term in Eqn. ( 66 ), we obtain
𝖪𝖫 ( p 0 ∥ q T ) \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(p_{0}\|q_{T})
≤ 𝖪𝖫 ( q T ∥ p 0 ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 𝔼 x t k ∼ q ← t k [ ∫ t k t k + 1 ∂ ∂ t 𝖪𝖫 ( q ← t | t k ( ⋅ | x t k ) ∥ p t | t k ( ⋅ | x t k ) ) d t ] \displaystyle\leq\mathsf{KL}(q_{T}\|p_{0})+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{t_{k}}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t_{k}}}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathsf{KL}\left(\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}(\cdot|x_{t_{k}})\|p_{t\mid t_{k}}(\cdot|x_{t_{k}})\right)\mathrm{d}t\right]
≤ e − T ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) \displaystyle\leq e^{-T}d\log(S)
+ ∑ k = 0 N − 1 𝔼 x t k ∼ q ← t k ∫ t k t k + 1 𝔼 x t ∼ q ← t | t k ∑ y ≠ x t [ Q ^ t ( x t , y ) − Q ← t ( x t , y ) + Q ← t ( x t , y ) log Q ← t ​ ( x t , y ) Q ^ t ​ ( x t , y ) ] d t \displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{t_{k}}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t_{k}}}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{t}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t\mid t_{k}}}\sum_{y\neq x_{t}}\left[\widehat{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)-\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)+\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)\log\frac{\overset{\leftarrow}{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)}{\widehat{Q}_{t}(x_{t},y)}\right]\mathrm{d}t

= 𝔼 x t ∼ q ← t [ ∑ y t : d_H ⁡ ( y t , x t ) = 1 h ( s T − t ( y t , x t ) ) ] − 𝔼 x ℓ ∼ q ← ℓ [ ∑ y ℓ : d_H ⁡ ( y ℓ , x ℓ ) = 1 h ( s T − ℓ ( y ℓ , x ℓ ) ) ] \displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x_{t}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{t}}\left[\sum_{y_{t}:\ham(y_{t},x_{t})=1}h\left(s_{T-t}(y_{t},x_{t})\right)\right]-{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{\ell}\sim\overset{\leftarrow}{q}_{\ell}}\left[\sum_{y_{\ell}:\ham(y_{\ell},x_{\ell})=1}h\left(s_{T-\ell}(y_{\ell},x_{\ell})\right)\right]

𝖪𝖫 ( p 0 ∥ q T ) \displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(p_{0}\|q_{T})
≤ e − T ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 ∫ t k t k + 1 ℒ SE ​ ( T − t k , s ^ T − t k , s T − t k ) + ( φ ⁡ ( T − t ) − φ ⁡ ( T − t k ) ) ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle\leq e^{-T}d\log(S)+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SE}}(T-t_{k},\widehat{s}_{T-t_{k}},s_{T-t_{k}})+\big(\varphi(T-t)-\varphi(T-t_{k})\big)\,\mathrm{d}t
≤ ε score + e − T ​ d ​ log ⁡ ( S ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 ∫ t k t k + 1 ( φ ⁡ ( T − t ) − φ ⁡ ( T − t k ) ) ​ 𝑑 t . \displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{score}}+e^{-T}d\log(S)+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\big(\varphi(T-t)-\varphi(T-t_{k})\big)\,\mathrm{d}t.
